No computed `send` (rule 1): every dispatch is a generated `case`. A `Runner.run` holding two scans of `app/controllers` stopped Spinel's type inference (rule 94), so each emitter scans for itself.
- Rejected: running the `included` block at boot (a stored block, rule 88); stubs alone (above); stubbing Relation's methods on the concern (a second return type for those names, rule 34); `ModuleName.per_page` as the documented spelling (spike item 3 showed `self.class.per_page` works); refusing a model's `include` of a module that is not a concern (the first design; Ruby's own `include` is left alone now, and a misspelt concern name is a `NameError` at load).
- Consequences: `include A, B` splices B, then A, as Ruby runs the hooks; a model concern's `class_methods` name may not be a scope of the model or one of `ModelScan::RESERVED_SCOPE_NAMES` (the instance-method clash cites `file:line`); an `include` inside a model concern's `included` body is expanded and spliced after the outer body; include positions are counted lexically, so a callback declared in a loop or a conditional above the `include` is miscounted (clamped); a model concern's callbacks run after the model's own of the same kind; `app/controllers` is scanned twice; a removed concern's `gen/concerns` file is deleted and its emptied directories go with it. D15's note that concern support was a future feature is amended.
- Verification: `test/gen_concerns.rb`, `gen_model_concerns.rb`, `gen_namespaced.rb`, `gen_controllers.rb`, `helper_method.rb`, `controller_views.rb`; spike item 3.

### D61. Controller additions and the encrypted session (2026-11-11, phase 4a)

- Decision, parameters (C-1): `Params#permit` takes a key (a String value), `key: []` (a list of Strings) and `key: [...]` (a nested level, recursively, bounded by the filter's depth, never the request's) and returns `Hash{String => Object}` (plan decision 15); a value of the wrong kind, an unlisted key and an upload are dropped, and a filter that is not an Array (or an Array inside the filter) raises `ArgumentError`, a programmer's error and not a 400. `Params#expect(article: [...])` returns the level under its one key and raises `ParameterMissing` (`"param is missing or the value is empty or invalid: article"`, answered 400) when it is absent, of the wrong kind or permits nothing; `expect(:id)` answers `{"id" => "3"}` and `expect(tags: [])` `{"tags" => [...]}`, one return type. TimeZone's private `expect` became `expect_byte` (plan decision 16).
- Decision, responses (C-2, C-3, C-9): `send_data(data, filename:, type:, disposition:, status:)` with `UploadedFile.content_disposition_utf8` (a `?`-fallback quoted name and RFC 5987's `filename*`). `fresh_when` / `stale?` set a weak ETag, `W/"<first 32 hex of SHA-256>"` of a String or of `"<table>/<to_param>-<updated_at epoch>"` for a record, each followed by the request's format and `Assets.build_digest` (a digest of the asset digests, the import map and, in a `--embed-views` build, the embedded views), and `Last-Modified`, and answer 304 to GET and HEAD only (a per-user page puts the user in the `etag:` String; a page with a CSP nonce is not made `public:`); a non-empty `If-None-Match` decides alone (RFC 9110 13.1.3), else a parseable `If-Modified-Since` (IMF-fixdate, parsed in `request.rb`'s own byte parser: the HTTP layer cannot use `Cast`). `expires_in` writes `Cache-Control`, which `fresh_when` keeps. A 304 carries no Content-Type, Content-Length or body. A response negotiated by `Accept` (no extension, an Accept header sent) carries `Vary: Accept`, merged into a Vary the action set. `STATUS_SYMBOLS` has every Rack 3 symbol and `Response::STATUS_TEXT` RFC 9110's phrases (plan decision 24), with five old aliases.
- Decision, `rate_limit` (C-4): `by:`, `with:`, `if:`, `unless:` name controller methods, reached through generated `rate_limit_key` / `rate_limit_condition?` tables and `run_callback`, and gen refuses a name with no `def`; `name:` tells two limits apart; `store: :cache` (the default) counts with `Cache.increment("rate-limit:<controller_path>:<name>:<key>:<now / within>", expires_in: within)`, fixed windows aligned to the epoch, `Retry-After = within - now % within` (plan decision 17); `store: :memory` keeps D26's per-process counter. A `name:` with `:` and a second limit of the same name in one controller's chain are refused (so no client-chosen key can land in another limit's bucket), a `by:` method answering nil or `""` raises instead of counting every client together, and a `with:` method that renders nothing is followed by the 429. `RateLimit.reset_all!` also deletes the cache's rate-limit rows. `cybertrain.rb` requires `cybertrain/cache`.
- Decision, the session and cookies (C-7, C-8, Q13): `require "cybertrain"` loads `cybertrain/encryptor`, so every program links Spinel's openssl package. `Crypto.derive_key(secret_key_base, label)` gives 32 bytes per purpose (`"cybertrain session"`, `"cybertrain signed cookie"`, `"cybertrain encrypted cookie"`, behind a fixed prefix so no derived key equals an HMAC the framework computes under the raw secret; plan decision 18). The session cookie is `Base64.urlsafe_encode64(Encryptor#seal_bytes(JSON, "session"), padding: false)`, AES-256-GCM with a fresh 12-byte nonce and the purpose as additional data (plan decision 19); a value that is empty, over 4096 bytes, not Base64, not authentic (a changed, truncated or foreign one, a 0.4 signed cookie, a `cookies.encrypted` value) or not a JSON object loads as an empty session, and a session whose cookie would pass 4096 bytes raises `Session::CookieOverflow` before any Set-Cookie. `Session.load` / `dump` take the derived key. `CookieJar` (`cookies`): plain, `permanent` (`Max-Age=631139040`), `signed` (`"<b64>--<64 hex>"`, the HMAC over `"<name>=<b64>"`, compared in constant time) and `encrypted` (additional data `"cookie:<name>"`), with `Path=/`, `HttpOnly`, `SameSite=Lax` and `Secure` like the session cookie; writes replace an earlier Set-Cookie of the same name (`Response#replace_cookie`), and a value written is the value read in that request (plan decision 20).
- Decision, transport (C-5, C-11): `Cybertrain::ContentSecurityPolicy` (one method per directive, Symbols for the keywords and schemes, String sources checked for spaces, `;`, `,` and control bytes at declaration) is declared in `config/app.rb`, and a controller's `content_security_policy do |p|` copies its nearest ancestor's or the application's policy, runs the block at once and keeps the copy by class name (plan decision 7). The `ContentSecurityPolicyHeader` middleware, installed only when a policy is declared, writes `Content-Security-Policy` (or `-Report-Only`) with a per-request nonce (16 random bytes, made once per Context) in the nonce directives, and none on a 304, whose headers would replace a cached page's. `Response.default_permissions_policy` (`camera=(), microphone=(), geolocation=()`, plan decision 23) joins the default headers. `SslRedirect` (plan decision 21): https by `X-Forwarded-Proto` (its first element), read whatever `trusted_proxy` says; 301 for GET / HEAD, 307 otherwise, to the request's `Host` once it passes `Config.host_name?` (else 400); HSTS `max-age=63072000; includeSubDomains`; the health path's GET / HEAD exempt; `Config#secure_cookies?` (session_secure or force_ssl). Host checks: `CYBERTRAIN_HOSTS` entries are appended to `allowed_hosts` after the loopback names, the check runs in development or when they are set (`Config#host_check?`, plan decision 22), the health path's GET / HEAD passes, and a refused Host is logged with its bytes escaped.
- Reason: what Rails gives every controller; the session encrypted by default as Rails' cookie store is (Q13), with no new secret.
- Spinel: rule 62 framework-wide once openssl is in every program (spike item 4): every byte scanner reads its parameter through a fresh local, and the grep for a boxed `getbyte` / `each_byte` dispatch prints 0 for test/application.rb's program and the blog's server after every task. One return type per name (rules 10 / 34 / 41): `permit` widens once to `Hash{String => Object}`; `expect` coexists with Mailer's (spike item 4); the template's `form_action` became `form_record_url` because the policy's `form_action` returns nil. `if:` / `unless:` are read with `binding.local_variable_get` (rule 95). The policy block runs once (rule 88). Messages are built into locals before `raise` (rule 58); polymorphic containers are seeded (rule 9). The two-process rate-limit test spawns its own binary and reaps it (rule 75).
- Rejected: a signed-only session (readable by the client); a new key variable (keys derive from `secret_key_base`, so rotating it signs everyone out as before); per-process counts by default (a deployment of N processes was N times looser, D26); requiring a trusted peer for `X-Forwarded-Proto` (every request behind an undeclared proxy would loop; a forged header only skips the forger's own redirect); a second hosts list (spec C-11 extends `allowed_hosts`); an automatic ETag on every response.
- Consequences: every 0.4 session ends once and `Session.load` / `dump` with the raw secret raise `Encryptor::Error`; every application links OpenSSL (the generated Dockerfile's build stage has `libssl-dev`, its runtime stage `libssl3t64`; macOS needs Homebrew's OpenSSL); `params.permit` values need narrowing before String methods; SessionStore builds a jar per request; a jar cookie over 4096 bytes of `name=value` raises `Session::CookieOverflow`, and `path:` / `same_site:` are written as given (CR and LF refused). D8's session paragraph, D25's and D26's are amended.
- Verification: `test/params.rb`, `send_data.rb`, `http_caching.rb`, `controller_status.rb`, `http_response.rb`, `rate_limit.rb`, `rate_limit_store.rb` (two processes), `session.rb`, `cookie_jar.rb`, `openssl_dispatch.rb`, `csp.rb`, `force_ssl.rb`, `host_authorization.rb`, `config_settings.rb`; spike item 4.

### D62. View additions: number helpers, forms, layouts, Turbo helpers, `time_tag` and `current_page?`, collections (2026-11-11, phase 4a)

- Decision, numbers (V-6): `Cybertrain::NumberHelper`'s six module functions (`number_with_precision`, `number_with_delimiter`, `number_to_human`, `number_to_percentage`, `number_to_currency`, `number_to_human_size`) are also template helpers with Rails' keywords. They take an Integer, a Float or a numeric String and round half up on the digits of its decimal text, never `Float#round` (plan decision 27), so both runtimes print the same; anything else raises `ArgumentError` naming the helper, and so does a precision outside 0 to 100 or an exponent past 400 (a request-supplied value must not build a string of that many digits). A nil renders `""` in a template (the wrappers answer before the formatter, as 0.4's `number_with_delimiter` did), while the module functions called from Ruby raise for it. Formats are English until 4b reads them from the locale.
- Decision, forms (V-7): `form_with` gains `scope:`, `data:` (validated attribute names), `local:` (accepted, ignored: Turbo decides, plan decision 26) and `model: [:admin, @article]`; the builder gains `select` (values, `[text, value]` pairs, groups and Hashes for `<optgroup>`, `include_blank:`, `prompt:`, `selected:`, `disabled:`, `multiple:` with its hidden empty input), `collection_select`, `radio_button`, the other input types and `check_box(include_hidden: false)`; the standalone `select_tag`, `options_for_select`, `options_from_collection_for_select`, `label_tag`, `text_field_tag`, `hidden_field_tag`, `submit_tag` share its option and attribute builders. Every attribute value and option text is escaped once, a SafeString as it is; the attribute order stays the house order, options are joined with `"\n"` and written `<option selected="selected" value="x">` (plan decision 25).
- Decision, layouts (V-9): gen reads a controller's (and its concerns') `layout "x"`, `layout false` and `layout :method` lines, with `only:` / `except:`, and writes `controller_layout(action)`; the first applicable declaration wins, a method answering nil falls back to the parent's layout, and any other argument (`nil`, a lambda, a constant) is refused. `render layout:` takes `true`, `false` or a name (a leading `layouts/` is stripped, as Rails' `_normalize_layout` does); a named layout that does not exist raises `MissingTemplate`, except the default `application`, which stays optional; non-html formats render without a layout (D59).
- Decision, Turbo (V-10): `turbo_frame_tag(id_or_record, src:, loading:, target:)` writes each attribute only when given (plan decision 26); in templates `turbo_stream.replace / update / append / prepend / before / after / remove` is a `Template::TurboStreamBuilder` reached through the interpreter, a block being rendered by the helpers before the call; in controllers `turbo_stream` is a `Cybertrain::TurboStreams` and `render turbo_stream:` takes one element or an Array, as `text/vnd.turbo-stream.html`. Both builders take exactly one of `partial:` (with `locals:`), `html:` and (in templates) a block.
- Decision, V-11 and V-13: `time_tag(time, text = nil, format:)` writes the xmlschema `datetime` and formats the text with `:short`, `:long` or a strftime pattern; `current_page?(path)` answers for GET and HEAD, ignoring a trailing slash, comparing the query only when the argument has one; `image_tag` is D63's. `render partial:, collection:, as:, spacer_template:, locals:` renders once per item with `<as>_counter` (given to a strict-locals partial only when it declares it); `render @records` picks each record's `<plural>/_<singular>` partial; a component whose one required keyword is named after it gets a generated `Klass.with_collection(items)` (`render component: "comment", collection: @comments`), and gen refuses a hand-written `def self.with_collection`.
- Reason: the helpers fin_dash's views use, in Rails' spelling and output.
- Spinel: `f.select` has no public Ruby method: `select` exists on Kernel and on every Enumerable with other return types (rule 34), so the template's `f.select` runs a private `select_field` (it is reachable from templates only). `TurboStreams#update` beside `Model#update` and the other action names have name-home rows. A strftime with a built pattern on a value that may be a Date returns the pattern (rule 96), so `time_tag` calls a literal strftime per directive and raises on an unknown one rather than pass it through. A component's name reaches the stored builder lambda as a fresh local (rule 97). Byte scanners read through fresh locals (rule 62). No block is stored (rule 88).
- Rejected: Rails' "a later `layout` replaces an earlier one" (the spec allows several declarations with `only:` / `except:`, and the first applicable is easier to read; listed under the differences from Rails); `data-disable-with` and CDATA around `javascript_tag`'s content; `fields_for` and nested attributes (no `accepts_nested_attributes_for`, spec section 13).
- Consequences: `f.check_box`'s hidden `"0"` has no `autocomplete="off"` (Rails 7 writes one); `time_tag` raises on a width or flag its strftime would read differently (`%6L`, `%-T`, `%:Y`) instead of printing something else, and a width on a number replaces its padding as in CRuby; `collection:` refuses another keyword next to it (a block's content is still dropped).
- Verification: `test/number_helper.rb`, `helpers.rb`, `form_builder.rb`, `form_tags.rb`, `controller_views.rb`, `gen_controllers.rb`, `turbo_helpers.rb`, `collections.rb`, `component_render.rb`, `gen_components.rb`.

### D63. Assets and the import map (2026-11-11, phase 4a)

- Decision, the build: every directory under `app/assets/`, then `app/javascript/` and `vendor/javascript/`, is a root, and a file's logical path is its path below its root; one found under two roots stops gen, naming both (plan decision 29). `spin run gen` writes each file as `public/assets/<name>-<digest>.<ext>`, the digest the first 8 hex characters of its SHA-256 (plan decision 2), once, through a temporary file and a rename; dotfiles and symlinked directories are skipped, a symlinked file leading out of its tree or nowhere stops gen, and so does a path with a space or a control character. A stylesheet's `url()` naming an asset is rewritten to its digested path, resolved against the stylesheet's logical directory as Propshaft resolves it (a leading `/` from the roots), its `?query` / `#fragment` kept; stylesheets are digested in dependency order and a cycle is refused; `data:`, `http(s):`, `//`, `#` and `%23` are left alone, and any other target that is not an asset is left as written with a warning. `gen/assets.rb` holds the logical-to-digested `case`, the import map's JSON and the preload list, and installs them in `Cybertrain::Assets`; the kept digests (the current one and the two before it per logical path; older files deleted) are listed in `public/assets/.manifest`, so `gen/assets.rb` stays a pure function of the sources and `--check` compares only it (plan decision 3). `Cybertrain::AssetManifest` is the one reader of `.manifest` and of digested names, for gen and for boot.
- Decision, the import map: `config/importmap.rb` is read as text: `pin name, to:, preload:` (a `to:` without a scheme is a logical asset path, a pin without `to:` names `<name>.js`) and `pin_all_from dir, under:, preload:` (`index.js` named after the directory); `preload:` defaults to true (plan decision 28); any other line stops gen.
- Decision, at run time: `asset_path`, `stylesheet_link_tag(name, media:, nonce:)`, `javascript_include_tag(name, nonce:)`, `image_tag(source, alt:, size:, class:)` and `javascript_importmap_tags(entry)`; a URL with a scheme, `//`, `data:` or `cid:`, and a path starting with `/`, are written as given. An unknown logical name raises `Assets::UnknownAsset` in development and test and, in production (`Assets.raise_on_unknown = false` at boot), is logged once (at most 1,000 names) and written undigested. `Static` serves a file under `/assets/` whose name carries a current or kept digest (`Assets.kept_file?`, the `.manifest` read at boot) with `Cache-Control: public, max-age=31536000, immutable`, everything else as before, no file whose real path leaves `public/` and no dotfile under `/assets` (`.manifest`, a crashed run's temp file; `/.well-known` is still served). Gen stops at an asset root (`app/assets`, `app/javascript`, `vendor/javascript`) that is a symlink. The development loop watches the three asset roots (`Dev::WATCHED`, now in `cybertrain/dev/watched.rb`). `cybertrain new` writes `app/assets/stylesheets/application.css` (formerly `public/style.css`), `app/javascript/application.js`, `config/importmap.rb`, the layout's `csp_meta_tag` / `stylesheet_link_tag` / `javascript_importmap_tags`, `/public/assets/` in `.gitignore`, the commented CSP block, OpenSSL in the Dockerfile (D61) and `asset_path: /app/public/assets` in `config/deploy.yml`.
- Reason: Rails 8's Propshaft and importmap-rails layout, with the manifest compiled into the binary so the deployed program needs only `public/assets/` beside it.
- Spinel: the manifest is a generated `case` (rule 1; spike item 5: a 200-arm `case` compiles in 1.3 s and answers in 293 ns, and SHA-256 of 1 MB takes 0.8 ms compiled). The resolver lambda is made by a method, not written as a literal into the module's ivar (rule 82); module state lives in module-level ivars (rule 18); byte scanners read through fresh locals (rule 62); a `[String, Array<String>]` pair returned and destructured compiles.
- Rejected: the kept-digest history in `gen/assets.rb` (`--check` would depend on the build's past); resolving `url()` against the file's directory under `app/assets/` (Propshaft resolves logical paths, so `url("../images/logo.svg")` from `stylesheets/` climbs out of the roots and is not an asset); `bin/importmap pin` and bundling Turbo (spec section 13); serving through a symlink out of `public/`; ETags on digested files (a digested file never changes).
- Consequences: `public/assets/` is build output, git-ignored, and `cybertrain build` and the image build run gen; a fresh image carries only the current digests, which Kamal's `asset_path` bridges; a Capistrano-style deploy that symlinks `public/assets` must mount it instead; a reverse proxy serving `public/` itself must add the immutable header; dotfiles under `/assets` (`.manifest`, a crashed run's temp file) are not served; examples/blog's stylesheet moved to `app/assets/stylesheets/application.css` with `url("logo.svg")`.
- Verification: `test/gen_assets.rb` (a fixture with a compiled, populated `gen/assets.rb`), `assets.rb`, `static.rb`, `dev_watcher.rb`, `cli_new.rb`; examples/blog's tests fetch the digested stylesheet and check the immutable header; spike item 5.

### D64. Shared state has one owner each, and the Application lifecycle is a contract (2026-11-11, issue #180)

- Context: `Application` takes a `Config`, yet `boot` wrote process-wide settings into `Views`, `Assets`, `Multipart`, `BlobStore`, `Params`, `Request`, `ParameterFilter`, `Response` and the logger, and `DB` holds one pool for the process. What happens on a second boot, on a second `Application`, after a stop, and what a test must put back between cases, was whatever the code happened to do.
- Decision: the framework's state is one of four kinds, and each kind has one owner and one period in which it may change.

  | Kind | What | Owner (who writes it) | Set | May change | Ends |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | Configuration | `Cybertrain.config` (a `Config`) | the application: the environment, then `config/app.rb` | when the program loads | until `Application#boot`; after it only what a role forces (`worker`, a port argument) | with the process |
  | Registrations | `Job` handlers, `Scheduler::TASKS`, `Background` services and feeds, `Main.command` commands, `Views.helpers_builder` / `component_builder`, `Assets.install`'s tables, `Config.encryption_required`, `ContentSecurityPolicy` controller policies | the files that load: `gen/*.rb`, `app/**`, `require "cybertrain/websocket"` | at load time | not after boot | never reset (a process is one program) |
  | Settings | `Views` (engine, layout, URL resolver), `Assets` (kept digests, raise on unknown), `Multipart` limits, `BlobStore.root`, `Params.max_page`, `Request.trusted_proxy` / `trusted_proxies`, `ParameterFilter.names`, `Response.default_permissions_policy`, the logger's level | the booting `Application`, through `Application.configure_views` and `Runtime.apply` and nothing else | `Application#boot` | by the next boot (the last boot wins, whole); a test may set one directly | `Runtime.reset!` restores the defaults |
  | Resources | the `DB` pool; the HTTP server; the threads of `Job`, `Scheduler`, `Background`, `Stream::Poller`; the TERM/INT/HUP handlers | whoever acquired it: a pool belongs to the boot that connected it (or to the caller who called `DB.connect`); a role's threads and handlers belong to that role | `boot` (the pool), `serve` / `run_worker` / `run_feeds` (the rest) | never shared between two roles | the role stops its own threads and gives the handlers back before it returns; `Application#shutdown` closes a pool its boot connected |
  | Request state | `Context`, `Request`, `Response`, the controller, the session, the transaction a thread holds (`DB.with` inside `DB.transaction`) | the request's thread | per request | within the request | with the request |

- One process runs one `Application` at a time. That is now said rather than implied: the settings are the process's, so two Applications with different configs cannot serve side by side, and several are not a goal (D35 runs several processes instead). What is supported, because tests and `Main` do it, is one Application after another.
- The lifecycle contract:
  - `boot` validates, connects the database unless something already has, and installs the settings. It answers `self`; `booted?` is true afterwards.
  - Booting twice (the same Application, or a second one) is defined: every setting is written again from the booting Application's config, so the settings after a boot depend on that boot alone. The pool is kept: a boot never replaces a connected pool (the first boot's, or the caller's).
  - `shutdown` (new) closes the pool only if this Application's boot connected it and it is still that pool (`DB.generation`); it is a no-op otherwise and when repeated. `boot` works again after it. The settings stay until the next boot or `Runtime.reset!`.
  - `serve`, `run_worker` and `run_feeds` stop every thread they started and restore the signal handlers they replaced before they return; the Application stays booted, and a role may be run again (`test/main_roles.rb` runs one after another). One role runs at a time (`Runtime.enter_role`): each owns the process-wide service threads and the signal handlers.
  - `Test::Client`, `console`, `migrate`, `db` and `Main.command` boot (or only configure views) and start nothing (D39).
- `Cybertrain::Runtime` (cybertrain/runtime.rb) is the one writer of the settings: `Runtime.apply(config)` (what `boot` used to do inline, every setting on every call), `Runtime.reset!` (every setting back to its default; for tests), `Runtime.describe` (all of them as one line, so a test compares two states with one assertion), `Runtime.applied`, `Runtime.role`. The modules keep their own setters (`Params.max_page=` and the like stay usable from a test), and `Cybertrain.config` and `DB.with` stay the entry points: `Runtime` holds no copy of a setting, so nothing is managed twice.
- Tests: there was no single way to put the settings back, so a case could pass or fail by what booted before it. `Runtime.reset!` is that way, and `test/runtime.rb` checks the order independence directly: boot A then B and boot B alone leave the same `Runtime.describe`, and the reverse; a reset after any boot equals the state before the first.
- Known gap, kept on purpose: an `Application` built with `config:` other than `Cybertrain.config` installs its own config's settings, while code with no Application at hand (`Job`, `Mailer`, `Stream`, `Encryptor`, `HttpClient`, `Password`, the template helpers) reads `Cybertrain.config`. `Main` always passes the global one, so only tests see two; a test that needs those modules to follow assigns `Cybertrain.config = c` itself. Making boot assign it would change what existing tests observe, and moving the readers onto an injected runtime object is the "execution environment object" the issue leaves for when several Applications become a goal.
- Rejected: a `Runtime` object passed to every reader (every `Params`, `Request`, `Multipart` call site would need one, and under Spinel a module-level ivar is the cheap, typed way to share a value: rules 18, 64); refusing a second boot (the test suite boots dozens of Applications per process); resetting the registrations too (they are written by `require`, which runs once); closing the pool at the end of `serve` (a process that served is about to exit, and an in-process caller may still read the database).
- Verification: `test/runtime.rb` (the defaults, a boot's settings, reset, order independence, double boot, `shutdown` and pool ownership, the role claim); `test/application.rb`, `test/main_roles.rb` and the rest of the suite run unchanged.
- Changed since by D79: `Runtime.apply` also writes I18n's four settings (`default_locale`, `available_locales`, `raise_on_missing`, and `mark_missing = !production?`) and `Runtime.reset!` puts them back (`:en`, `[:en]`, false, true); `Runtime.describe` leaves them out, as `I18n` answers them itself.
- Changed since by D81: `Runtime.apply` also sets `Clock.zone` from `time_zone`, `Runtime.reset!` puts UTC back, and `Runtime.describe` ends with `time_zone=<name>`.

### D65. Config: checks that only read, and file operations kept apart (2026-11-11, issue #181)

- Context: `Config` (1,700 lines) held the settings of every area (web, database, jobs, feeds, stream, security), the checks boot runs on them, the resolution of the secret (which reads, creates and chmods `tmp/secret_key`) and a `mkdir -p`. `Application#boot` called fourteen `*_error` methods one by one, with `resolve_secret!` after them. Nothing said which of these may touch the disk, so "is this configuration usable" could not be asked without preparing the disk for it.
- Decision: the boundary is the side effect, not the class.
  - `cybertrain/config/validation.rb` holds every check (`session_same_site_error`, `csrf_exempt_prefixes_error`, `max_body_bytes_error`, `upload_limits_error`, `scheduler_error`, `max_page_error`, `trusted_proxies_error`, `health_error`, `feeds_error`, `stream_error`, `security_error`, `encryption_error`: one per area, unchanged, under their public names) and `Config#validation_error`, which asks them in the order boot always did and answers the first message or `""`. The contract of that file: a check reads the Config (and, for `scheduler_tz`, the system's zone files) and does nothing else: no database connection, no file or directory created, no secret resolved.
  - `cybertrain/config/files.rb` (`Cybertrain::ConfigFiles`, private) holds the file operations: `load_or_create_secret(path, length)` and `make_directory`. `Config#resolve_secret!` keeps its name and its behaviour and is now two lines over it; `Config.make_directory` stays for its callers (`DB::CLI`).
  - `Application#boot` states the order (D66): `validation_error`, then the registrations' check (`Scheduler.job_kinds_error`), then `resolve_secret!`, then the database.
- Every setting name, default, environment variable and message is unchanged; the areas' methods are the ones tests already call (`test/application.rb`, `test/config_settings.rb`, `test/health.rb`, `test/boot_encryption.rb`). One ordering changed, on purpose: a cron task naming an unregistered job kind is now reported after all of the Config's own problems rather than between `scheduler_error` and `max_page_error` (it is a registration's problem, not the Config's), so a boot with both problems names the Config's first.
- Spinel: the class is reopened in a second file rather than handed to a validator object: each check reads ivars whose types the class already settles (rules 7, 9), and a `validate(config)` taking the Config through a parameter would read the same values through accessors for no gain. The module-level `@encryption_required` and its accessors stay in config.rb, where the class body first runs (rule 18).
- Rejected: one `Config` class per area (`WebConfig`, `JobConfig`, ...) behind `config.jobs.threads` (every setting name applications and the guides use would change, for a split that the per-area check methods already give the validation); collecting every error instead of the first (boot prints one line and exits; a list is a different contract for `cybertrain doctor` to ask for when it needs it); resolving the secret lazily in `SessionStore` (the first request would create a file, and a read-only deploy would fail there instead of at boot).
- Verification: `test/config_validation.rb` (a valid default; the order; each area's boundary values through `validation_error`; validation with paths that do not exist leaves no file, no directory and no database connection; `resolve_secret!` creates the file once, mode 0600, and refuses production without a secret), next to the existing per-area tests.
- Changed since by D79 and D80: `validation_error` also asks `i18n_error` (the locale names) and `auth_error` (the `auth_*` limits), after the stream endpoint and before the security headers; both only read.

### D66. Boot in three stages, and a failed start releases what it acquired (2026-11-11, issue #182)

- Context: `boot` validated, resolved the secret, connected the database and wrote the process-wide settings in one run of statements, and `serve`, `run_worker` and `run_feeds` started their threads one after another with the stops after the wait. Nothing said what a failure halfway left behind. No leak had been reproduced; the contract was missing.
- Decision: `boot` is three stages, and each stage's failure has a defined outcome.
  1. **Validate** (`validate_boot`): `Config#validation_error` (D65), the embedded-views check and `Scheduler.job_kinds_error`. Reads only. A problem prints `error: MESSAGE` and exits 1, as before; nothing has been acquired, so nothing is released.
  2. **Acquire** (`acquire_boot_resources`): the secret (the one file operation, D65) and the database pool, connected only when none is, and then remembered as this boot's (D64).
  3. **Apply** (`apply_boot_settings`): the views (the embedded templates are parsed here; one that does not parse raises) and `Runtime.apply`.
  - An exception in stage 2 or 3 propagates to the caller unchanged, after `abandon_boot`: the pool is closed if this boot connected it (a pool that was already connected is never touched), the Application is not booted, and, if stage 3 had begun, `Runtime.reset!` puts the settings back to their defaults, so a half-applied set is never left in place. A retry therefore starts from a defined state: no pool of this boot's, default settings. The unwinding is an `ensure` with a flag, not a `rescue`: under Spinel a `rescue StandardError` does not see every exception class (rules 33, 61), and an `ensure` does.
- The roles follow the same rule for their threads: what a role started is stopped when a later start raises, not only after a clean wait.
  - `serve`: the stream poller, the listener, the scheduler, the job threads and the background services are started inside a `begin`; its `ensure` stops all of them (each `stop!` is a no-op for what never started) and releases the role. `PortInUse` still prints the boot error and exits 1.
  - `run_worker` and `run_feeds` already restored the TERM and INT handlers in an `ensure`; the same `ensure` now stops the scheduler and the job threads, or the feeds started before the one that failed, and the feeds role's supervisor flags.
- Ownership, so that a release never closes what it did not open: the pool belongs to the boot that connected it (`DB.generation` tells a later pool from it); a caller's pool, a caller's signal handlers and a caller's logger are given back as they were. Threads belong to the role that started them; `Test::Client`, `console`, `migrate`, `db` and `Main.command` start none.
- Rejected: turning stage-1 failures into exceptions (`boot` is called by `Main` at the top of a process, and `error: ...` with exit 1 is the documented outcome that tests and deploy scripts read); restoring the previous Application's settings after a failed stage 3 (a snapshot of every module's state would be a second copy of the settings, which D64 rules out; defaults are defined and a re-boot restores the rest); a generic "resource stack" object (four fixed resources in a fixed order read better as four lines in an `ensure`, and a Proc list crosses the boundaries rules 67 and 69 are about).
- Verification: `test/boot_stages.rb`: a database that cannot be opened and an embedded template that does not parse each leave `booted?` false, no pool, default settings, and a second boot with the fault removed succeeds; the same failures with a pool the caller connected leave that pool open; a feeds role whose second service raises on start stops the first, restores the handlers and frees the role. `test/runtime.rb` covers double boot and `shutdown`.

### D67. Role runners: `Application` assembles, a runner per role operates (2026-11-11, issue #183)

- Context: `Application` (760 lines) assembled the configuration and the middleware stack, was the entry for a request (`call`), and also ran three roles: the starts, the waits, the stops, the signal handlers, the banners, and the development loop's rebuild, restart monitor and `execv`. A change to how a process is operated and a change to what the application is made of met in one class.
- Decision: `Application` keeps the composition and the request entry (`boot`, `stack`, `call`, `shutdown`, `server`) and its public role methods, which now delegate; one object per role owns the operation.
  - `Roles::ServerRunner` (cybertrain/roles/server.rb): the listener, the stream poller, the scheduler, the job threads and the background services, the TERM trap, the boot banner; and, in development only, everything of D12 (the rebuilder, the watcher, the HUP trap, the restart monitor, the `execv`). The rebuild is the server's and nothing else's, so it lives there rather than in a shared base.
  - `Roles::WorkerRunner` (cybertrain/roles/worker.rb): the scheduler and the job threads, TERM and INT.
  - `Roles::FeedsRunner` (cybertrain/roles/feeds.rb): the named feeds and their supervisor, TERM and INT, the exit status after a feed died too often, and `feeds status`.
  - `Roles` (cybertrain/roles.rb) holds only what the three really share: the stop flag (`Roles.request_stop`, `Roles.wait_stop`; `Application.request_stop_roles` stays as its public name), the role banner and the `error:` line. There is no runner base class: the three share a shape, not code, and a base method calling subclass hooks is a Spinel hazard (rules 3, 88).
  - Each runner's `run_*` method reads top to bottom as the role's order: what starts, in which order, what the wait is, what stops, in which order. That order is now in one method per role and nowhere else.
  - `Main` still only parses the first word and dispatches: `Application#run`, `#run_worker`, `#run_feeds`.
- Kept: every command, setting, banner line and exit status; TERM (and INT for `worker` and `feeds`) as flags only; the handlers given back; the partial-start unwinding of D66; `SPINEL_WORKERS` set before the first thread (rule 22); `Application#server` (tests read its settings), `Application.spinel_workers`, `.serving_workers`, `.spinel_workers_for`, `.environment_banner`, `.port_argument`, `.name_problem`, `.feed_status_line`, `.status_now`.
- `Test::Client` drives `Application#call`, the console and `Main.command` configure views only: none of them builds a runner, so none can start a service. `test/boot_stages.rb` asserts it (`Job.running?`, `Scheduler.running?` and `Stream::Poller.running?` stay false and no role is claimed after a boot and a request).
- Rejected: runners that own the boot (`boot` is the Application's: `Test::Client` needs it without a role); one `Runner` with a role parameter (the three differ in every line but the trap); moving the roles into `Main` (`Main` would then know Job, Scheduler, Background and the development loop).
- Verification: `test/main_roles.rb` (the roles, the signals, the handlers, the exit statuses), `test/dev_application.rb` (the development loop), `test/application.rb` (the server's settings, the banner), `test/feeds_exit.rb`, `test/boot_stages.rb`; examples/blog's tests and CI's end-to-end job start the real server.

### D68. Job split into registry, store, retry policy, executor and worker (2026-11-11, issue #184)

- Problem: `cybertrain/job.rb` (1454 lines) held the public API, the handler registry, every SQL statement, the claim, the run loop, the heartbeat thread, recovery and the retry arithmetic in one module, so the storage format, the stop behaviour and the retry policy could not be read, changed or tested apart.
- Decision: `Cybertrain::Job` stays the only surface an application, the scheduler, the mailer, `/jobs` and generated code call; every `Job.*` method keeps its name, signature, default arguments and YARD comment, and its body is now one delegation. The module keeps the registry itself (`Handler`, `Run`, `HANDLERS`, `handle`, `register`, the lookups and `process_label`), and four parts sit behind it, each a module of class methods in `cybertrain/job/`:
  - `Job::Store` (`store.rb`): every statement on `cybertrain_jobs` and nothing else: `install!`, `insert`, `claim(now, label)`, `write_outcome`, `touch_heartbeats(now, label, ids)`, `requeue_stale` (with `fail_exhausted`), `backfill_keys`, the counts and the prunes. It starts no thread, never sleeps, calls no handler and does not know whether a pool runs. The claim is still one `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` transaction (the concurrency count, the `SELECT` and the guarded `UPDATE` under one write lock) and the outcome still one `UPDATE` fenced by `claimed_by` and `attempts`: the statements moved, none was rewritten, and the schema is untouched.
  - `Job::RetryPolicy` (`retry_policy.rb`): the arithmetic only: `default_wait` (60, 300, 900, 3600 s), `policy_wait` (the base, or `attempts ** 4 + 2`), `exhausted?`, `recovery_limit(own, default)` and `error_text` (cut at 1000 characters). Every input is an argument; the file requires nothing and reads no database, Config or clock.
  - `Job::Executor` (`executor.rb`): one attempt on the calling thread: `Store.claim`, the handler call with its rescue arms, the decision (`RetryPolicy`), and the outcome write tried up to three times when the database raises (`record_outcome`; the rescue that used to wrap the SQL now wraps `Store.write_outcome`). It keeps the ids in flight (`Executor::IN_FLIGHT`, formerly `Job::IN_FLIGHT`), which the heartbeat reads.
  - `Job::Worker` (`worker.rb`): the pool: `start!`, `request_stop`, `stop!`, the job loops, the heartbeat thread, the generation counter and the drain. A loop calls `Executor.work_one`; the start's recovery and the heartbeat go through `Job.recover!`, `Job.backfill_keys` and `Job.heartbeat!`, so the file holds no SQL.
- Reason: the three things the issue wants changeable apart now have one file each, and the delivery guarantees are a property of `Store` alone: a reader checking that a claim is atomic, or that a stale worker cannot overwrite a reclaimed row, reads two methods and no thread code.
- Unchanged on purpose: at-least-once delivery and everything D38 and D39 say; the log lines; the order of operations in `start!`, `enqueue`, `work_one` and `recover!`; `Job.backoff`, `Job.handler_index`, `Job.ensure_installed`, `Job.in_flight_ids`, `Job.live_loops`, `Job.fail_outcome_writes`, `Job::BACKOFF`, `Job::MAX_ERROR_LENGTH` and `Job::OUTCOME_TRIES`, which tests, the scheduler and `/jobs` use, stay as delegations or aliases. One lock became two (`Executor::FLIGHT_LOCK` for the ids in flight, `Worker::POOL_LOCK` for the live-loop count and the stop time); neither is ever taken inside the other, as before.
- Spinel: a module nested two deep (`Cybertrain::Job::Worker`) keeps its state in module-level ivars and compiles as `Job` did (rules 18/64); the four files are required at the top of `job.rb`, before `module Job`'s body, so `Job::BACKOFF = RetryPolicy::BACKOFF` holds under CRuby too (rule 17), and they name the registry only inside methods. "Nothing claimed" is a `Run` whose `run_id` is -1 rather than a nil (rules 11/56). Each rescue stays in a plain method (rules 32/45), and the delegating `Job.retry_later` / `Job.discard`, which generated blocks call, pass their arguments one level down unchanged. No new constraint was found.
- Rejected: instantiable classes with the store injected into the worker (a second store does not exist, an object boxed behind an interface costs Spinel a dispatch on every claim, and module state must be module-level ivars anyway); a `Job::Registry` module (the registry is the public registration API and `Job::HANDLERS` is read by the scheduler and by tests, so moving it would only add an alias); handing the limits and keys to `Store.claim` as arguments so that the store would not read the registry (three more parameters on every call for a dependency that is data, not control: the store still starts nothing and calls no handler); moving the public documentation onto the parts (the API reference documents `Job.*`).
- Verification: `test/job_boundaries.rb` (new) runs `RetryPolicy` with nothing else in the program, under CRuby too, and reads the sources: `store.rb` names no thread, pool or handler call, and `worker.rb`, `executor.rb` and `job.rb` contain no SQL. The existing tests pass unchanged against the split: `test/jobs.rb` (claim and outcome fences, two claimers under two labels, stale-heartbeat recovery and its exhaustion, an outcome write that fails three times and one that fails once), `test/job_threads.rb` (several job threads, the heartbeat thread, the drain), `test/gen_jobs_compiles.rb`, `test/mailer_job.rb`, `test/scheduler.rb`, `test/main_jobs.rb`, `test/main_roles.rb`, `test/jobs_page.rb`. One contract was missing and was added to `test/job_threads.rb`: a job that becomes due after `request_stop` is claimed by no thread and stays `queued`.
- Changed since by D78 (three concurrency fences, none of them a change to the split): `Store.claim` takes the caller's ticket as a third argument, `Store.touch_heartbeats` takes the claims in flight (id and attempt number) instead of the ids, and `Executor` keeps the attempt number beside each id in flight.
- Changed since by D79: `Executor.run_handler` sets `I18n.locale = nil` before it calls the handler, so each job starts at the default locale whatever the job before it on the thread set (and whether that one raised).

### D69. The model generator validates before it emits (2026-11-11, issue #185)

- Decision: generating `gen/models/` is three stages with a typed value between each: `ModelScan` reads `app/models/*.rb` as text into `ModelInfo`s and refuses a declaration it cannot read; `Gen::ModelValidation` (`generator/model_validation.rb`) checks those against `db/schema.rb` and against each other and answers one `ValidatedModel` per table; `ModelsEmitter.emit_validated` writes Ruby from it and refuses nothing. `Gen::Runner` runs the three in that order, so a model that cannot be generated stops gen before any file is written.
- Decision, what moved: every semantic check `models_emitter.rb` and `association_emitter.rb` held: a table without columns, column names (`check_column_names`), a binary column's default, a scope named like a column, `encrypts` on a missing or non-text column and `uniqueness:` on an encrypted one, the if: / unless: and `validate` method names, and the whole association planning (the declared and foreign-key-derived `AssociationPlan`s, their reader names, `dependent: :nullify`, `counter_cache`, the cache ivars). `ValidatedModel` carries the resolved result (the table, the plans, the encrypted columns, the scopes, the view methods left to `call_view_method`, the validation names, the counter columns), so the emitter no longer sees a `ModelInfo` or the other tables. The names a model spells (`reader_name`, `model_class_name`, ...) moved below both stages into `Gen::ModelNaming`; `ModelsEmitter` still answers them.
- Decision, diagnostics: the wording and the exception classes are unchanged (`ModelScan::Error` with `app/models/<file>:<line>` for a declaration, `ArgumentError` for the schema). A schema diagnostic now starts with `db/schema.rb:<line>: ` when gen runs on an app: `Cybertrain::Schema` is built by running the file and its tables carry no line, so `SchemaPlaces` finds the `create_table` line, or the first line of that block naming the column, in the file's text; a column no line spells (`t.references :post` for `post_id`) gets its table's line, and without the text (a schema a test defines) the message is as before.
- Reason: the emitter held the checks beside the code they guard, so a new declaration touched recognition, validation and emission together, and a check could be exercised only by emitting a whole model. With the boundary a check is a function of the scan's values and the schema and is tested without generated source.
- Spinel: the stage is part of `bin/gen.rb` and compiles as written; `ValidatedModel` holds only monomorphic fields (a `Schema::Table`, Strings, typed Arrays), and a typed empty Array of it is declared with `ValidatedModel.placeholder` (the `Array.new(0) { ... }` idiom). The new method names avoid ones the program already answers with another type (rules 10, 34, 41; `script/check-reserved-names`): `source_table`, `validate_table`, `SchemaPlaces.in_app`. The separate scan passes Runner makes for the controllers and concerns sources stay separate (their comment in runner.rb: type inference did not converge with the results shared).
- Rejected: a diagnostics list collected across models instead of the first error raised (every caller and snapshot expects the raise, and gen's exit path is the uncaught error); a new exception class (the two existing ones are what tests and users match); recording lines in `Cybertrain::Schema` itself (the DSL runs under Spinel, which has no `caller`, and the schema is also loaded by the running app); validation calling into `ModelsEmitter` for names (the stage would depend on the one after it).
- Left for later: the same boundary for the other generators. `controllers_emitter.rb` and `concerns_emitter.rb` still raise for what is known only once the scans are put together (a `helper_method` or `rate_limit` method no controller, parent or concern defines, a `helper_method` named like a framework helper, a concern and its including class defining the same method), and `assets_emitter.rb` for an asset path leaving `public/assets`; their scans (`ControllerScan`, `ConcernScan`, `HelperScan`) already place their own errors by file and line. The model concerns' checks are in `ModelScan` (a concern counts as written at its `include`) and did not move.
- Verification: `test/gen_model_validation.rb` (the stage alone, compiled: the validated values, each diagnostic with its file and line without emitting, `SchemaPlaces`, and `outputs_for` equal to `outputs`); `test/gen_models.rb`, `gen_runner_models.rb` and `gen_models_compiles.rb` unchanged, the last compiling and running the fixture's generated models against SQLite; `examples/blog/gen` and the generated files under `test/fixtures/` byte-identical.

### D70. Controller: rendering and HTTP caching behind internal boundaries, execution order pinned (2026-11-11, issue #186)

- Decision, the facade: `Cybertrain::Controller` keeps every method an application or generated code calls (`render`, `respond_to`, `render_template`, `partial_body`, `view_env`, `controller_layout`, `fresh_when`, `stale?`, `expires_in`, `Controller.layout_path`, the callback declarations), with the same signatures. What it no longer holds is the work behind three of them, which changed for three unrelated reasons in one 1,778-line class.
- Decision, rendering: `Cybertrain::Rendering` (`cybertrain/rendering.rb`, `@api private`) is a module of functions over plain values: the template environment (`page_env`, `stringify_locals`), which template and layout a name and a format select and the rendered body (`page_body`, `partial_html`), the format an action that rendered nothing answers in (`implicit_format`, `accepts_html?`), and the body of `render json:` / `turbo_stream:` / `layout:` (`json_text`, `turbo_stream_text`, `layout_named`). The controller gathers what only it has (its `view_assigns`, its directory, the layout its `layout` lines chose, the helpers built around it, the request) and writes the returned body to its response. `Rendering` names no controller, callback or response, so it cannot know that callbacks exist. `Cybertrain::MissingTemplate` is defined there.
- Decision, HTTP caching: `Cybertrain::HttpCache` (`cybertrain/http_cache.rb`, `@api private`) is a module of functions over a `Request` and a `Response`: `set_validators` (ETag from a String or a record, the format and the build digest; Last-Modified; the default Cache-Control), `validators_fresh?` (RFC 9110 13.1.2 and 13.1.3), `lifetime` (`expires_in`'s header) and `add_vary_accept`. `fresh_when` and `stale?` call the first two and answer the 304 themselves; `run_action` calls `add_vary_accept` where it called its own method.
- Decision, execution: callbacks, the implicit render and exception handling stay in `Controller` (`run_action`, `run_before_callbacks`, `run_after_callbacks`, `run_one`, `rescue_with_handler`), unmoved. Their order is pinned instead, by `test/controller_order.rb`: before callbacks, the action, the implicit render, the after callbacks, then `Vary: Accept`; a before callback that renders stops everything after it but the Vary; an exception in a before callback, the action or an after callback goes to its `rescue_from` handler with whatever had not run yet skipped, the handler working on the response as the action left it (headers kept, a render replaced) and its Vary still added; a handler that renders nothing leaves the response unperformed, with no implicit render after it; an exception in the implicit render, or one without a handler, skips the after callbacks and propagates.
- Reason: each of the three has its own reason to change (a template feature, a caching rule, the callback machinery), and the first two are pure enough to read and test without a request cycle. The split is by that, not by line count: `controller.rb` still holds the long documented public surface, which is what a facade is.
- Spinel: both collaborators are module functions, not objects holding the controller: a render allocates nothing for them, no block or Proc crosses the new boundary, and no method is called on a boxed controller. `respond_to` stays in `Controller` because it calls its block (twice, for the HTML fallback), and a Proc handed one level further down loses its parameter's class (rule 69); for the same rule, and because a callback is either a stored block called with `self` or a `run_callback` / `rate_limit_key` `case` that gen writes into each subclass, the execution loop cannot move to another object without passing `process`'s Proc two levels or dispatching generated overrides on a boxed receiver, with a `rescue => e` around it (rules 32, 45). The function names are the modules' own (`page_body`, `set_validators`, not a second `render` or `fresh?`): one return type per method name across the program (rules 10, 34, 41).
- Rejected: a renderer object built per request around the controller (an allocation per render, and it would know the controller, so "does not know callbacks" would rest on discipline rather than on what it can reach); moving `respond_to` and `FormatCollector`'s driving into `Rendering` (rule 69, above); a `CallbackRunner` (above; revisit only if Spinel lifts rule 69's two-level limit); `HttpCache` under `cybertrain/http/` (it needs `Model`, `Assets` and the inflector, and that layer must stand alone); keeping `Controller.vary_lists_accept?`, `Controller.turbo_stream_body` and the private helpers as delegations (nothing outside the class called them; `Controller.layout_path` stays because gen/controllers.rb emits calls to it).
- Consequences: with no views configured, `render_template` and `partial_body` now build the environment and the helpers before raising `MissingTemplate` (the check moved into `Rendering` with the engine); the message and the class are unchanged. `ATTACHMENT_RESERVED_ACTIONS` keeps the names of the removed private methods: refusing them as attachment names costs nothing and a later delegation cannot clash.
- Decision, three corrections after the move (2026-11-11, found by the review of pull request #210 in code the move had not changed, so made in a change of their own): `validators_fresh?` answers an `If-None-Match` that lists `*` before it looks at the response's ETag, so a response validated by `last_modified:` alone, which has none, is fresh for it (RFC 9110 13.1.2; it answered 200); an ordinary entity tag still needs the response's ETag to match. `set_validators` takes a sixth argument, whether the request may be answered in HTML instead of its own format, and keys the ETag on `<format>+html` then; the controller asks `Rendering.accepts_html?` and passes the answer, so `HttpCache` still names nothing of rendering. `accepts_html?` reads the q-values (D59 has both of these and their compatibility paragraph). The order `test/controller_order.rb` pins is unchanged, `Vary: Accept` included.
- Compatibility (`If-None-Match: *`): what changes: a GET or HEAD with `If-None-Match: *` to an action that calls `fresh_when` / `stale?` with `last_modified:` and no `etag:` gets `304` where it got `200` with the body. Who notices: a client that sends `*` on a read, which is rare (it is the precondition of a create-only PUT); such a client asked exactly for this answer. What to do: nothing, or stop sending the header to get the body. Minor release, with the D59 changes.
- Verification: `test/controller_order.rb` (new), `controller.rb`, `controller_views.rb`, `controller_formats.rb`, `controller_status.rb`, `http_caching.rb`, `csp.rb`, `turbo.rb`, `turbo_helpers.rb`, `component_render.rb`, `helper_method.rb`, `template_integration.rb`, with unchanged snapshots; examples/blog's tests.

### D71. What a template may read from a model, and where the contract lives (2026-11-11, issue #187)

- Problem: `cybertrain/template/interpreter.rb` reopened `Cybertrain::Model` to add `attribute_or_method?(name) = true`, so the view layer defined part of the model's contract, and because nothing overrode it a mistyped name on a generated model (`<%= post.titel %>`) rendered as an empty string, the same as a column whose value is nil (the known gap of section 13).
- Decision, the contract: the template layer asks a record for exactly this, and `cybertrain/model.rb` declares all of it (the interpreter no longer reopens `Model`): `id`, `persisted?`, `new_record?`, `to_param` and `errors`, answered by `Model` itself, and by name `read_attribute(name)` (a column), `read_association(name)` (the record of a `belongs_to` / `has_one`, the records of a `has_many` as an Array) and `call_view_method(name)` (a public argument-less method of `app/models/<model>.rb` or of a concern it includes), in that order. The three return nil both for a nil value and for a name they do not know; `attribute_or_method?(name)` tells the two apart. Templates only read: no hook takes an argument beyond the name, writes, saves or queries by a computed name, and what an Array, a String or another value answers is the interpreter's own per-type table as before.
- Decision, the generator: `gen/models/<model>.rb` now defines `attribute_or_method?` as a `case` over the names the three readers of the same file answer, one `when` per group (`:id` and the columns, a renamed column under both its names as in `read_attribute`; the associations; the view methods), `else false`. The base `Model#attribute_or_method?` stays true, so a hand-written subclass that does not declare its names (the framework's tests have many) keeps every unanswered name a nil and compiles unchanged.
- Decision, the diagnostic: a name a generated model does not have raises `Cybertrain::Template::RuntimeError` with the message every other undefined method in a template has, `<template>:<line>: undefined method '<name>' for <Model>` (`posts/show:3: undefined method 'titel' for Post`), which the development error page and the log already show. `f.collection_select` / `options_from_collection_for_select` reading such a name raise `ArgumentError`, `<helper>: <Model> has no attribute or view method '<name>'`, as they already did for a model that answered false.
- Compatibility: this is a behaviour change for applications, in its own commit. After `spin run gen`, a template that reads a name the model has neither as a column, an association nor a view method fails at render (HTTP 500 in production) where it used to print nothing. The cases to look for when upgrading: a typo; a column since dropped or renamed in a migration; a method that takes an argument, is private, or is defined outside `app/models` (none of these was ever callable, they rendered empty); a method of `Model` outside the five above (`post.model_name`, `post.attributes`, `post.save`). Migration: fix the name, or define the argument-less public method in the model; `<% if post.respond_to?(...) %>` does not exist, so a template shared by two models must only read names both have. A name that exists and is nil renders exactly as before, and nothing changes until gen is run again (the generated files are committed).
- Outside a request (`Views.render_partial`, `render_mail`, `render_component`): none of the four hooks needs a request, so a record reads the same there, an unknown name included. What differs is the helpers, built with no controller: path helpers work (`url_resolver` is process-wide), a form renders without its authenticity token, and the request-bound helpers (`csrf_token`, `csrf_meta_tags`, `params`, `flash`, `request_path`, `*_url`, and an application helper that reads one of them) raise `Views::OutsideRequest`, which passes through the interpreter without the `name:line:` prefix.
- Rejected: flipping the base default to false (every hand-written `Model` subclass would have to list its names to render a nil column, for no gain to applications, whose models are generated); making `read_attribute` raise for an unknown name (it is public API that returns nil, and `FormBuilder` reads virtual attributes such as `:password` through it); a distinct "missing" sentinel returned by the readers (a fourth value in every polymorphic slot, and the three readers would still need the name list); wrapping the record in a view-model object (an allocation per record read and a second surface to keep in step with the four hooks); a new error class for the unknown name (the existing message already carries the template, the line, the name and the model, and a new class would change what `rescue` clauses and the error page match).
- Verification: `test/template_model_contract.rb` (the fixture app's generated `Post` / `Comment` against SQLite: a nil attribute, an unknown name, a `has_many`, a `belongs_to` present and absent, view methods including a concern's, a private method, the same through `Views.render_partial`, `OutsideRequest` beside a record read, and a hand-written model on the base default); `test/gen_models.rb` (the emitted `case`, a renamed column's two keys); `test/gen_models_compiles.rb` (the fixture is fresh); examples/blog's tests.

### D72. `Relation#dup`: branching queries from one base; chaining stays mutating (2026-11-11, issue #188)

- Context: a Relation's chain methods (`where`, `not`, `where_sql`, `where_like`, `where_between`, `or`, `order`, `order_sql`, `limit`, `offset`, `distinct`, `none`, `having_sql`, `includes`, `preload`) and `page` change the relation they are called on and return it (D43's "Rejected: copy-on-write relations"). ActiveRecord returns a new relation from each, so code ported from Rails that keeps a base query and chains from it twice (`recent = base.order(...)`, `approved = base.where(...)`) gets one relation under three names, and the second query silently carries the first one's conditions. The behaviour was documented, but there was no supported way to branch.
- Decision, the copy: every generated `<Model>Relation` has `dup`, which answers a new relation of the same generated class holding the same chain state: the where terms and their binds, the order, limit and offset (so a `page` window too), `distinct`, `none`, the HAVING text and its binds, and the `includes` names. Nothing runs; the copy is as lazy as its source, and its `to_a` / `first` / `find_by` / scopes are typed by the model as the source's are. `base.dup.where(...)` is the branch; `base.dup.page(n)` windows the copy. The model class does not answer `dup` (`Article.all` is already a fresh relation).
- Decision, the contract: after `copy = rel.dup`, no chain method, `or` or `page` called on either changes what the other selects, in either direction. The copy owns its Arrays (`@wheres`, `@binds`, `@having_binds`, `@includes`): `Relation#copy_chain_to` hands the source's state to `take_chain` / `take_having` on the new relation, which copy element by element into the Arrays its own `initialize` typed. The elements are shared, which is safe because a Relation never changes one in place: a term or order text is a String that is only appended to the Array or replaced, and a bind is nil, an Integer, a Float, a String or a `DB::Blob` that is only read. A copy of a `preloaded` relation (a has_many reader after `includes`, whose Array is the owning record's association cache) answers from a new Array of the same records and keeps answering from memory until one of its own chain methods drops it; the records are the same objects, as with `to_a`. This differs from ActiveRecord, where `dup` of a loaded relation is unloaded: here the preloaded records are what makes `article.comments` free after `includes`, and a copy that queried again would turn a branch in a loop into an N+1.
- Decision, chaining stays mutating in this change. Making it non-destructive is a compatibility change that is not decided here; the copy is what that change would be built on, and it had to be proven first.
- Reason for `dup` rather than a new name: it is the spelling a Rails developer reaches for, and before this change it already compiled and did the wrong thing: the built-in `dup` of an object copies the ivar slots, under Spinel as under CRuby, so `rel.dup` had its own limit and order but the source's very `@wheres` and `@binds` Arrays (NOTES rule 99; `test/relation_dup.rb` fails in seven of its twelve tests with the generated method removed). Defining the method closes that trap instead of leaving it beside a `copy` that works.
- Reason for keeping the chain mutating now: (1) existing code depends on it in a way no compiler error would reveal. A chain call whose result is discarded, usually under a condition, is the natural way to build a query on a mutating relation, and the generator itself emits it (`unique_value_taken?`: `rel.where({ scope_attr => scope_value }) unless scope_attr.nil?`, then `rel.exists?`); under non-destructive chaining each such line becomes a no-op and the query silently widens, which for a uniqueness check, a tenant filter or a `delete_all` is worse than the pitfall being fixed. (2) `page` is specified to window its receiver and return a `Page` (D23: `@page = @articles.page(...)`, then the template iterates `@articles`); it cannot return both, so a non-destructive chain would leave one mutating method or need a new pagination API. (3) The cost is small but not nothing: a copy is one relation, five Arrays and the two `DB::Blob` seeds of the typed bind Arrays; measured compiled on the fixture models, a four-step chain takes about 2.5 µs to build and about 3.5 µs with a copy after every step. That would be paid by every query to protect the few that branch.
- Spinel: a base-class method that returned the copy would be typed `Cybertrain::Relation` (rule 5), so `dup` is emitted per model and constructs `<Model>Relation.new("<table>")` by name; the base class only moves state (`copy_chain_to` and the two `take_` methods return nil, like the setters). The state crosses as method arguments, not through new readers, so no accessor name is added to every relation (rules 10 and 34); the polymorphic bind Array is read by index into an Array seeded with its element types (rule 9), as `merge_or` already does. Overriding `dup` on a user class compiles and is dispatched statically on the generated type; `PostRelation#dup` and `CommentRelation#dup` returning different classes is the `where` precedent.
- Release policy, should chaining become non-destructive later: it is a behaviour change of the public API, so it ships only in a minor release (0.x.0 before 1.0, never a patch), announced one minor release earlier as deprecated in that release's notes and in the guide's "differences from Rails" list, with this section updated; the generator, the framework and `examples/blog` are converted in the announcing release, so `spin run gen` output already holds no statement-form chain call when the switch lands.
- Migration steps for an application, valid from this release on because both spellings mean the same under either behaviour: (1) where one relation feeds several queries, write `base.dup.<chain>` for each branch (or build each from the model class); (2) where a chain call's result is discarded, rebind it: `rel = rel.where(...) if cond` in place of `rel.where(...) if cond`. To find the second kind, list chain calls that start a statement and are not assigned: `grep -rnE '^\s*[a-z_@][A-Za-z0-9_.@]*\.(where|not|where_sql|where_like|where_between|or|order|order_sql|limit|offset|distinct|none|having_sql|includes|preload)\b' app lib | grep -vE '^\S+\s*[a-z_@][A-Za-z0-9_@]*\s*='`, and read each hit (a line that is a method's last expression returns the relation and needs no change). To find the first kind, look for a relation held in a local or ivar and used on two or more later lines. (3) `page` keeps windowing its receiver unless the announcing release says otherwise. After the switch `dup` stays, as a plain copy.
- Rejected: making every chain method copy now (the reasons above); a base-class `dup` (typed as the base, rule 5); a new name such as `copy` or `branch` beside a `dup` that still compiles to a shallow copy; deep-copying terms and binds (nothing mutates them; it would double the allocation); dropping the preloaded records in the copy as ActiveRecord does (an N+1 where the source had none); a class-level `Article.dup`.
- Consequences: generated `gen/models/*.rb` gain seven lines per model, so applications regenerate (`spin run gen`); hand-written Relation subclasses (the older shapes in some tests) have no `dup` unless they define one, and the built-in shallow copy is what they get. A String passed to `order_sql` is kept as given, as before, so mutating that String afterwards changes every relation holding it; this is not new and `dup` does not copy it.
- Verification: `test/relation_dup.rb` (compiled, on the fixture's generated models: one test per piece of state, in both directions, statement counts for laziness and for the preloaded copy, typed readers and a scope on the copy); `test/gen_models.rb` (the emitted text); `test/gen_models_compiles.rb` (the fixture is fresh); `examples/blog`.

### D73. The decimal contract: a Float exact to 15 significant digits, rounding on the decimal digits, a generated range validation, and minor units for money (2026-11-11, issue #189)

- Context: D42 made a `t.decimal` attribute a Float rounded with `Float#round(scale)` and left `precision` to the DDL, to be revisited "when Spinel's `bigdecimal` can be built from a Float or String, round, and compare". That recorded a reason, not a contract: nothing said which values survive a write and a read, how a tie rounds, what a value outside the declared precision does, or what a sum answers, and an application keeping money in a decimal column could not tell whether Rails' guarantees held. Issue #189 asked for a technical verification first, and for no immediate change of the type.
- Verification (spikes/decimal/, compiled with Spinel 2026.11.04.7026 against SQLite 3.45.1; each file records its output): (1) The exact types exist now. The `bigdecimal` package builds from a String or an Integer, has `+ - * /`, comparison and `round(n, mode)`, and compiles beside `cybertrain/db`; `Rational` compiles; Integer is 64-bit and raises `RangeError` past it. BigDecimal still has no `to_s("F")`, no `to_d`, no construction from a Float, no NaN or Infinity (NOTES rule 100). (2) The storage is the limit, not the Ruby type. A `DECIMAL(p,s)` column has NUMERIC affinity in SQLite: a bound text `"1234567890.12345678"` is converted to a REAL and reads back as 1234567890.12346, a whole REAL is kept as an INTEGER, NaN becomes NULL, Infinity is stored as `Inf`. An exact decimal attribute would need a TEXT or INTEGER column, which is a storage format change, and SQL `SUM` / `ORDER BY` / comparisons over TEXT would no longer be numeric. Rails on SQLite has the same floor (it writes REAL values, as the models guide's takeover section notes). (3) A decimal of up to 15 significant digits written as a Float and read back is the same decimal, at scale 2 and at scale 8: 0 of 20,000 values differ at 13, 14 and 15 digits, about 3,000 at 16, about 17,000 at 17. (4) `Float#round(scale)` in the shape `Cast.round_scale` had rounds the binary product under Spinel: 1.005, 1.015, 0.285 were stored as 1.0, 1.01, 0.28, where CRuby (and Rails, through BigDecimal) answer 1.01, 1.02, 0.29; and under both runtimes it moved a 17-digit value that needed no rounding. (5) `SUM` over REAL cells is a Float sum; SQLite 3.43 and later compensate it (1000 x 0.1 summed to exactly 100.0, and 100,000 random prices to the exact total), an older SQLite need not. (6) `SUM` over an INTEGER column is exact and raises `integer overflow` past 64 bits instead of wrapping.
- Decision, no type change: a decimal attribute stays a Float in a `DECIMAL(p,s)` column, with the contract below. BigDecimal as the attribute type is not adopted now: it would be exact in Ruby and still a REAL in the database, every caller would need text helpers the package lacks, and the read would have to construct it from a Float, which the package refuses.
- Decision, the contract of `t.decimal :x, precision: p, scale: s`:
  - Range and precision: with `p <= 15`, every decimal of `s` fractional digits with an absolute value below `10**(p - s)` is stored and read back as itself (the Float nearest to it; `number_with_precision(x, precision: s)` prints the digits that were written, and `where(x: value)` finds it). Beyond 15 significant digits (a `precision` above 15, or no `precision`) a value is the nearest Float, exact to 15 significant digits and no further: `99999999999999.99` reads as `99999999999999.98`. `decimal(18,8)` is therefore exact up to 9,999,999.99999999, not up to its declared 10 integer digits.
  - Rounding: a value is rounded to `s` digits where it is stored (`save`, `update`, `update_columns`, `update_all`, `insert_all`, `insert_all!`, `upsert_all`), half away from zero on the decimal digits of the Float's shortest text (`Cast.round_scale`, since this issue through `Cast.round_digits` and the number helpers' digit arithmetic): 1.005 is 1.01, -1.005 is -1.01, 2.5 at scale 0 is 3.0. It is the same under CRuby and Spinel, idempotent at any magnitude, and what Rails stores for the same input. The attribute in memory keeps what was assigned until the record is reloaded. Without a `scale` nothing is rounded.
  - Input: nil and `""` are NULL; an Integer or a numeric String is converted with `to_f` (`"abc"` is 0.0 and `"12.5 dollars"` 12.5, as `String#to_f` reads them).
  - Out of range: gen writes `validates :x, numericality: { greater_than: -10**(p-s), less_than: 10**(p-s) }` (`allow_nil: true` on a nullable column) for every decimal column that declares a precision, so `save` and `update` answer false with `X must be less than 100000000` instead of storing a value the column does not declare. The line is written between `model_concern_insert_at(0)` and `model_concern_insert_done`, so it does not count as one of the model's own `validates` calls and a model concern's rules keep their position (D60; the generated date presence of D42 is not wrapped so, which by the same reading shifts them; not tested here). The same comparisons refuse Infinity (`"1e999"` from a form) and NaN, which were stored before as `Inf` and as NULL. The bound is checked on the assigned value, so one within half a unit of the last place below it rounds up to the bound and is stored. The writers that skip validations (`update_columns`, `update_all`, the bulk writes) skip this too, as in Rails: there a non-finite value is stored as SQLite stores it, and `to_json` of such a record raises `JSON::GeneratorError`. A decimal without a precision is not checked at all.
  - Aggregates: `sum_float`, `average`, `minimum_float`, `maximum_float` and their `_by` / `_sql` forms answer a Float (`average` and the minimum and maximum nil over no rows, `sum_float` 0.0). A minimum or maximum is one stored value, so it is exact under the range above. A sum or average is a Float computation: round it to the scale at the edge with `Cast.round_scale(total, s)`; the rounded sum is the exact one while the total itself stays within 15 significant digits. `pluck_float` reads NULL as 0.0.
  - JSON: `as_json` hands the Float to `JSON.generate`, which writes a JSON number (`19.9`, not Rails' String `"19.9"`).
- Decision, money: keep it in an integer column of minor units (`t.integer :price_cents`, or `t.bigint`), not in a decimal. It is exact in Ruby (Integer arithmetic to 64 bits, `RangeError` past them) and in SQL (`sum_int` is exact and raises on overflow), and needs nothing the framework lacks: `(Cast.round_scale(params[:price], 2) * 100).round` reads a form field (round, never `to_i`: `(19.99 * 100).to_i` is 1998), and `number_to_currency(price_cents / 100.0)` displays it, exact to 15 digits of cents. Two helpers would shorten that (`Cast.minor_units(value, scale)` and a `minor_units:` keyword on the number helpers that shifts the digits without a Float); they are proposed, not built: nothing here needs them yet, and a decimal column within the contract above stays a supported choice for prices that are not summed into ledgers.
- Decision, the migration from a decimal column to minor units (the storage and the return type both change, so it goes in three steps and never rewrites a column in place): (1) a migration adds the integer column and fills it, `add_column :products, :price_cents, :integer` (with `null: false, default: "0"` for a NOT NULL price) then `execute "UPDATE products SET price_cents = CAST(ROUND(price * 100) AS INTEGER) WHERE price IS NOT NULL"` (`10**s` for another scale); check it with `SELECT COUNT(*) FROM products WHERE price IS NOT NULL AND price_cents / 100.0 <> price`, which must answer 0 (the spike's 100,000 rows did; a row it counts held more digits than the scale, or more than 15, and is decided by hand). (2) Run `spin run gen`, move the application to `price_cents` (forms and displays as above; a JSON API that exposed `price` keeps the key by writing `price_cents / 100.0` itself), and, while binaries of both versions run, keep the old column current from a `before_save` (`self.price = price_cents / 100.0`). Rolling back is deploying the previous version: the decimal column is intact. (3) Once no running version reads it, a second migration does `remove_column :products, :price`. A change in the other direction, or to another scale, is the same three steps with the arithmetic reversed.
- Reason: closer to Rails where the difference was silent (the tie rounding, a NaN stored as NULL), and explicit where it cannot be closed (15 digits, Float sums): an application can now read off whether its column is inside the exact range, and gets a validation error instead of a stored value when it is not.
- Spinel: `Float#round(n)` answers differently on a narrowed Float in a method than on a Float local (NOTES rule 100), so the framework no longer calls it for a stored value; BigDecimal, Rational and the 64-bit Integer as measured above.
