- Rejected: BigDecimal attributes now (above); storing decimals as TEXT to keep every digit (a storage format change that makes SQL arithmetic and ordering textual, and a Rails database would no longer be readable in place); a `Money` value object or a `t.money` column type (D42's reason stands: every caller converts, and the integer column already works); `Rational` (no column maps to it); raising from `Cast.round_scale` for a non-finite value (a form field would answer 500, the reason it passes them through; the validation reports it on the record instead); a generation-time warning for a precision above 15 (fin_dash's `decimal(18,8)` prices are well inside 15 digits, and it would warn on every run); rounding on assignment as Rails does (the generated writers are plain `attr_accessor`s by NOTES rule 43).
- Consequences: two behaviours change for an application built on 0.4. A stored tie follows the decimal digits (1.005 at scale 2 is now 1.01; compiled 0.4 binaries stored 1.0), for new writes only: existing cells are not rewritten. And `save` refuses a decimal outside its declared precision or not finite, where it stored it. `cybertrain/cast.rb` now requires `cybertrain/number_helper.rb`. The models guide's "Decimals are Floats" section still gives the revisit condition of D42 and `total.round(2)`; it should point here and to `Cast.round_scale` when the guides are next revised.
- Verification: `test/decimal_contract.rb` (the generated Post of the fixture app: save, read, update, `update_columns`, `update_all`, `insert_all`, `upsert_all`, the four aggregates, JSON, the validation, minor units), `test/cast_types.rb`, `test/bulk_writes.rb`, `test/gen_models_compiles.rb`; spikes/decimal/ for the measurements.

### D74. One SQLite file for every service: the storage boundaries, their transaction guarantees and the measured interference (2026-11-11, issue #190)

- Context: business tables, `cybertrain_jobs`, `cybertrain_cache` (the `rate_limit` counters among its rows), `cybertrain_messages`, `cybertrain_leases`, `cybertrain_schedules` and `cybertrain_feeds` share the application's database (D10, D37, D39, D52, D54, D56). That gives one file to back up and "enqueue iff the order committed", and it makes every service's write compete for SQLite's one writer. Until now nobody had measured how much, and which guarantees depend on the sharing was written down only per service.
- Decision: everything stays in the one file. No schema, API or behaviour changes. What this decision adds is the measurement ([bench/mixed](../bench/mixed), [bench/mixed_load.rb](../bench/mixed_load.rb)), the document that records it, the scale it supports and how to observe the interference ([docs/sqlite-shared-load.md](sqlite-shared-load.md)), and the boundary table below.
- The measurement: one compiled program on one WAL database with the framework's pool and the real poller, scheduler and job threads runs business transactions, primary-key reads, `Job.enqueue` (the job threads run the jobs), `Cache.write` / `read`, the `Cache.increment` of `rate_limit`, and `Stream.broadcast` (the poller hands the rows to a subscriber), each alone and then together, and prints operations, latency percentiles, job delay (enqueue to start), stream delay (broadcast to hand-off) and the calls that raised `database is locked`. Recorded on 2026-11-11 on a busy shared machine, so indicative: about 500 to 750 commits a second in all (each commit syncs the WAL, `synchronous = FULL`); the services divide that budget, so the business writers kept a half to a sixth of their throughput in the mixed phase; nothing failed, and the cost is tail latency (a business transaction's p99 from 8 ms to 64 ms on two workers, to 0.7 s at saturation); reads slow down too, on one worker because an FFI call holds it (NOTES rule 80) and at saturation because writers waiting for the lock keep the pool's connections; the job delay is `job_poll_interval` (0.7 to 0.8 s at the median even alone) and the stream delay `stream_poll_ms` plus the wait for a commit.
- The storage boundaries. "Joins" means the module's writes go through `DB.with`, which inside a `DB.transaction` on the same thread uses that transaction's connection (`Pool#with`, `held_index`), so the write commits or rolls back with the caller's rows. bench/mixed_load.rb checks it on every run for a business row, a job, a message and two cache rows (none after a rollback, all after a commit); `test/jobs.rb` has the job case.

| store | tables | all SQL lives in | joins the caller's transaction, and what relies on it | durability | if it moved out of the file |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| business data | the application's tables, `schema_migrations` | the generated models, `Relation`, `DB`; `DB::Migrator` | it is the transaction | must survive | not considered |
| jobs | `cybertrain_jobs` | `Job` (the queue, claim, outcome, recovery), `JobsPage` (the lists and the retry / discard / delete actions on failed and discarded rows) | yes: `Job.enqueue`, `perform_later` and `Mailer.deliver_later` in a transaction exist iff it commits. The claim is its own `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` transaction (two claimers never take one row or the last concurrency slot); the handler's writes and the outcome are separate commits, so a job runs at least once | must survive: a lost `queued` row is lost work | enqueue-in-transaction is lost: define the outbox (a business-side row and a relay), the duplicate rule for a relay that retries, and what `Job.enqueue` does when the queue is unreachable (raise, or queue locally) |
| scheduler timetable | `cybertrain_schedules` | `Scheduler::Timetable` | not used in one: written after a task ran. A crash between the run and the write can run a cron slot again on takeover | should survive (a lost row means a missed slot is not caught up) | nothing transactional is lost; it must stay readable by every process that can lead |
| cache, rate-limit counters | `cybertrain_cache` | `Cache`; `RateLimit.clear_shared_counts` (one `DELETE` of the `rate-limit:` key range) | yes, which matters as a hazard, not a guarantee: a `Cache.write` or a `fetch` miss inside a transaction that rolls back is not stored. `rate_limit` counts in a before-action, outside any transaction. `increment` and `write(unless_exist: true)` are atomic single statements, across processes | safe to lose, with two cautions: losing the counters reopens every window, and a `write(unless_exist: true)` used as a lock is released by the loss | the first candidate to move (a second SQLite file would take its commits off the writer lock). Define: reads and writes when the store is unavailable (a miss and a no-op, or an error; `rate_limit` open or closed), and that nothing may depend on a cache write rolling back |
| stream messages | `cybertrain_messages` | `Stream` (the poller and the hub call it, and run no SQL of their own) | yes: a broadcast in a transaction is delivered iff it commits. The ids are the delivery order and the replay cursor (`Last-Event-ID`); with one writer, id order is commit order, so the poller never passes an id that commits later | safe to lose after `stream_retention` (60 s); inside it a loss is a missed live update, and the client reloads | broadcast-in-transaction is lost: define whether a message may be delivered for a rolled-back change or dropped for a committed one, who assigns the order and the replay cursor, and what the endpoint does when the store is down (today: 503 until the poller runs) |
| leases | `cybertrain_leases` | `Lease` | not used in one. `acquire` is one upsert: of the processes sharing the file exactly one holds a name, because they share the writer | safe to lose (the next tick takes it again); the cost of a loss is two leaders until the old one's next renewal | it must be the same store for every process; an external one needs expiry by its clock and a rule for a leader that cannot reach it (stop leading) |
| feed status | `cybertrain_feeds` | `WebSocket::FeedTable` | not used in one | safe to lose (rewritten every `feed_status_interval`); `/up` reads it | `/up` and `feeds status` need a reader for it, and a rule for "unreadable" (today: not stale, one WARN) |

- The rule for a store outside the file: none is added now. One may be added (a second SQLite file, or an external service) only together with a written definition, in this document, of (1) its consistency with the business transaction: what replaces "in the same commit", from the table's last column; (2) unavailability: what each public call does when the store cannot be reached, and whether a request fails or degrades; (3) partial failure: the business commit succeeded and the store's write did not, and the reverse; (4) ordering and duplicates, for jobs and messages; (5) how the operator sees each of those. A second SQLite file attached to the same connection does not keep the guarantee either: in WAL mode a transaction across attached databases is atomic per file, not across them.
- Reason: the measured ceiling is far above the applications CyberTrain is for (tens of writing requests and jobs a second), the same-commit guarantees are used by the documented patterns (enqueue and broadcast from an action after a save), and each store that leaves needs a failure design that the single file makes unnecessary. The cheap levers come first and are in the document: do the service writes inside the request's transaction (one commit instead of several), `store: :memory` for limits that may be per process, fewer cache writes on request paths, batched broadcasts, a larger `pool_size`.
- Rejected: moving the cache to its own file now (its writes and the rate-limit counts were half of run A's mixed commits, but it is a behaviour change, needs the definitions above and a decision on backup and `db` commands, and no application has reported the limit); `PRAGMA synchronous = NORMAL` as part of this change (the largest single lever, since every commit is a sync; it changes what survives a power loss, so it needs its own decision and measurement); counting lock waits in `Connection#step_waiting` now (proposed in the document; it adds state to the per-statement path, which must be measured under two workers first, NOTES rule 57); a `.expected` snapshot for the bench (its numbers are not deterministic); `CacheStore` / `JobStore` / `MessageStore` classes (each store's SQL already sits in one module, two for jobs; an interface with one implementation would be an abstraction for a move that is not planned).
- Consequences: the limits are known and stated per deployment as a commit budget; an operator has the request log, the services' error lines, `/up`, `jobs list`, the jobs page, SQL and `bench/mixed`, and no direct count of lock waits (the proposed counter). a split of `cybertrain/job.rb` into several files does not change the table: the owner stays the job modules.
- Verification: `bench/mixed duration=5`, `... workers=2` and the saturation run of docs/sqlite-shared-load.md, compiled with `spinel bench/mixed_load.rb --strict-require -I . -O 1` (Spinel 2026.11.04.7026); each run printed `transaction coupling: ok (rows after a rollback: 0, after a commit: 5 of 5)` and no busy or error count. `script/gate` passes; no test or snapshot changed.

### D75. The HTTP layer's contract is one page, and each line names its test (2026-11-11, issue #191)

- Context: the server is the framework's own HTTP/1.1 implementation (D5, D6, D36), so which parts of HTTP it speaks, what it refuses and what it leaves to the reverse proxy are the framework's decisions. They were spread over D5, D14, D35, D36, the comments of `Server` and `HttpParser`, and the tests. No vulnerability prompted this; the list was missing.
- Decision: [docs/http-contract.md](http-contract.md) is that list: who owns what between the proxy and the server (TLS, HTTP/2, chunked request bodies, compression and connection limits are the proxy's), the request cases (versions, the request line, targets, line endings, header lines, the head limit, `Content-Length` framing, every `Transfer-Encoding` refused with 411, `Expect: 100-continue`, gzip bodies, the parameter limits), the responses (framing, HEAD, 304, the defaults, keep-alive, pipelining, errors), and when each kind of connection ends (an ordinary response, a streaming one, an upgrade, shutdown and the drain). Every row names the test that pins it; a row without one is a claim, and the known deviations are marked (a 204 carries `Content-Length: 0`).
- The inventory found the existing tests covering nearly all of it. Added to `test/server.rb`, only where a decision had no test: `Transfer-Encoding` beside `Content-Length` (411, and the bytes behind it never read as a request), `Transfer-Encoding: identity`, a request pipelined behind a rejected one, `Expect: 100-continue` (no interim response, the body awaited), a head ended by bare LFs, an absolute-form target, HTTP/1.1 without `Host`, and HTTP/1.0 with `Connection: keep-alive`. None changes the server: they pin what it does.
- The dependency boundary is unchanged: `script/check-http-layer` stays in the gate, and the page says so.
- Rejected: enforcing `Host` in the server (the stack's `HostAuthorization` owns the host, and a bare `HttpHandler` user may not want it); answering `100 Continue` (one more write path for a case proxies absorb); decoding chunked request bodies (the proxy buffers them; a second framing path is where smuggling bugs live); fixing the 204 `Content-Length` here (a wire change belongs in its own change, with its snapshots).
- Verification: `test/server.rb`, `test/http_parser.rb`, `test/http_request.rb`, `test/http_response.rb`, `test/response_stream.rb`, `test/stream_endpoint.rb`, `test/websocket_server.rb`, as the page cites them.

### D76. Spinel workarounds are indexed by shape, and a compiler update has a checklist (2026-11-11, issue #192)

- Context: spikes/NOTES.md numbers the Spinel rules in the order they were found and docs/spinel-feedback.md follows what went upstream; both are complete and neither answers "which shapes of the framework exist because of Spinel, which of those are permanent, and what must be re-checked when the pin moves". A general-purpose refactoring that is correct Ruby can stop the framework compiling, and nothing said that a CRuby pass is not acceptance.
- Decision: no new record, an index over the existing ones. spikes/NOTES.md gains "Workarounds that shape the design, and what a Spinel update re-checks": (1) the permanent shapes, which are API policy that follows from ahead-of-time compilation (explicit receivers, generated code, one return type per method, module-level ivars, typed seeds) with the decision and the test for each; (2) the workarounds tied to a build (rescue placement, the JSON rescue order, byte scanners, lambdas returned by methods, stored blocks and subclass overrides, the separate generator passes, sockets, `SPINEL_WORKERS`, `exit` on the main thread) with the rule, the files, the test that fails without it and its status on the pin; (3) the steps of a pin update (everything compiled, the benchmark, re-check the "still there" rows, read the release notes, remove a workaround only in its own change with its test); (4) what CRuby cannot tell you; (5) what a built binary still depends on at run time (OS and libc, `libsqlite3` and the versions the framework's SQL needs, OpenSSL 3, zlib, optional jemalloc, zoneinfo, a local file system), in step with docs/guide/install.md and the deploy tutorial.
- CONTRIBUTING.md and the pull request template say it as a rule: a structural change (code moved between methods, classes or files, a new abstraction, different generator output) is accepted only when the affected tests pass compiled under Spinel.
- Rejected: a second document repeating the rules (they would drift); renumbering the rules by topic (they are cited by number across the repository); a script that greps for workarounds (a shape is not greppable; the tests named in the index are the check).

### D77. Semantic differences from Rails are a contract with tests, and changing one has a procedure (2026-11-11, issue #193)

- Context: the framework uses Rails' vocabulary and ERB's syntax, the templates are a language of their own, and APIs such as `DB.transaction` carry contracts that come from Spinel. The guides list what differs feature by feature. For porting, the dangerous cases are the names that exist on both sides with another meaning, and nothing tied each of those to a test or said how one may change.
- Decision: [docs/compatibility.md](compatibility.md), over the existing guides rather than beside them.
  - The APIs whose meaning differs (transactions, the mutating relation chain and `dup`, decimal as Float, the typed `pluck` and aggregates, Integer ranges, `order`, callbacks that take their receiver, `new_action`, the first `layout` winning, String-only sessions, the JSON rescue, `filter_parameters`, CSRF by path, String cache values, job arguments, build-time concerns), each with where it is explained and the compiled test that pins it. Relation (D72) and decimal (D73) keep their own decisions; the page only indexes them.
  - The template language's contract: what the reference lists is what is supported, with no fallback to Ruby; every unsupported construct is rejected with the template, the line and the construct; three error stages (parse, render, lookup) with their classes and when each surfaces; unknown names are errors (helper, method, model attribute per D71, strict locals), `@ivar` being the one documented exception.
  - Development, test and production must mean the same thing. `test/template_parity.rb` renders the same pages through the file engine uncached, the file engine cached and the embedded table and compares the bytes, and makes sixteen failing templates fail in all three with the same class and message. The three differences that remain (the template's name in a message, when a syntax error surfaces, picking up an edit) are listed and tested.
  - What a compatibility change is, and its procedure: its own issue and pull request, separate from refactoring; a "Compatibility" paragraph in the decision; a minor release, never a patch; an "Upgrading from 0.x" entry in the guides; a generator diagnostic or a boot warning one minor release ahead where one is possible; a `db` command for a stored format; the `gen/` diff for generated code.
- Rejected: a machine-readable compatibility matrix (the tests are the machine-readable part); promising Rails' semantics wherever a name matches (most differences are Spinel's, D4, D9); semantic versioning's major-only rule before 1.0 (releases are 0.x; the minor is the unit applications already upgrade by, as the 0.4 guides show).
- Verification: `test/template_parity.rb` (20 cases); every other row cites an existing test.

### D78. Job queue: the heartbeat, a waiting claim and the drain clock are fenced like the outcome (2026-11-11, found in review of pull request #210)

- Problem: three interleavings of the job pool's own threads, all older than D68's split (which moved the code unchanged) and all read off the code, not seen in production:
  1. The heartbeat was fenced by `claimed_by` and the row's id, the outcome by `claimed_by` and `attempts`. An attempt that outlived its claim (its heartbeat went stale, the row was recovered) kept its id in flight; when the same process claimed the row again, the old attempt's entry went on refreshing the new claim's `heartbeat_at`. If the new attempt then left flight without an outcome (the outcome write failed three times), the row stayed `running` with a fresh heartbeat for as long as the old attempt ran, and recovery could not release it.
  2. A job thread checked the pool's stop flag before `Store.claim` and not again. The claim waits for SQLite's write lock (up to the 5 s of rule 80); a `Job.request_stop` during the wait was not seen, and after it the thread claimed and ran a job, possibly one enqueued during shutdown, inside or past the drain.
  3. `@stop_seen_at`, from which `stop!` counts `job_drain_timeout`, was stamped by any ending thread. After a `stop!` that timed out and a `start!`, a job thread of the old pool that finished later (or its heartbeat thread, late to wake) stamped it after `start!` had cleared it; the next `stop!` then counted the drain from that old time, found the deadline passed and did not wait for the new pool's jobs.
- Decision, the heartbeat: a claim is (`claimed_by`, id, `attempts`) everywhere. `Executor` keeps `IN_FLIGHT_ATTEMPTS` beside `IN_FLIGHT` (two Integer arrays of one length, changed together under `FLIGHT_LOCK`), `Executor.in_flight_claims` copies the pairs under one lock, and `Store.touch_heartbeats(now, label, claims)` matches `state = 'running' AND claimed_by = ? AND ((id = ? AND attempts = ?) OR ...)`. `Job.in_flight_ids` and `Job.heartbeat!(now)` keep their signatures.
- Decision, the waiting claim: `Store.claim(now, label, ticket)` asks `Job.claim_open?(ticket)` as the first thing inside its `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` transaction, with the write lock held, and takes no row when the answer is no. A job thread's ticket is its pool's generation (`Worker.claim_safely` passes it through `Executor.work_one_under`), and the claim is open while `Worker.pool_live?(generation)`; `Job.work_one(now)` from a test or a `Main.command` passes `Executor::NO_TICKET` (-1), which is always open, so a direct call still works with no pool running. The store does not learn what a ticket is: it carries an Integer back to the facade, as it reads `Job.limits?`, and `test/job_boundaries.rb` still finds no thread or pool word in it.
- Decision, the drain clock: `Worker.note_stop_seen(generation)` stamps only when `generation` is the current one, and `start!` steps `@generation` and clears `@stop_seen_at` in one `POOL_LOCK` section (`Worker.begin_generation`), the lock the stamp takes. A thread of an earlier generation can therefore neither stamp after the clear nor between the clear and the step.
- What is not closed, on purpose: a stop requested after the claim's check and before its COMMIT. That claim is one that happened just before the stop: the job runs and `stop!` drains it like any job running when the stop came. The check narrows the window from the whole lock wait to a few statements under the lock; closing it entirely would need the stop to take the database's write lock, from a signal handler.
- Unchanged: the schema; every public `Job.*` name, signature and default; at-least-once delivery (a claim refused under the lock wrote nothing and the row stays `queued`; a heartbeat withheld from a claim not in flight only lets recovery run the job again, which is the guarantee's direction); the lock order (`FLIGHT_LOCK` and `POOL_LOCK` are never held together, and neither is held across a database call).
- Rejected: one in-flight array of `[id, attempts]` pairs or of a struct (the registry and the store already keep parallel typed arrays, the shape Spinel types without boxing, rules 9 and 56; a nested Array or a new class buys nothing here); handing `Store.claim` a block or a Proc to call under the lock (a block called from inside `DB.transaction`'s own block, a yielding method with a rescue, is the shape rules 32 and 45 warn about); checking the stop in `Executor` after the claim returned and writing the row back to `queued` (a second write, an attempt counted for a job that never ran, and a window in which the process dies with the row `running`); stamping the stop time in `request_stop` (it runs in a signal handler: no clock call, no lock); a per-generation stop time (one pool exists at a time, the generation check says the same with one Float).
- Spinel: nothing new. `begin_generation` and the stamp are `Mutex#synchronize` blocks over module-level ivars, the shape `loop_started` already had; the in-flight removal searches with a `while` whose condition ends it, not a `break` inside the `synchronize` block.
- Verification, compiled: `test/jobs.rb` (the same-process reclaim case now also beats from the stale attempt: it touches no row and the second claim's `heartbeat_at` is unmoved); `test/job_threads.rb`, two new cases: a job thread waiting in the claim while the test holds the write lock, a `request_stop` during the wait, and the due row still `queued` afterwards (the existing "no claim after stop" case keeps its only thread busy and never has a claim pending); and a `stop!` that times out, a `start!`, the old thread ending, then a `stop!` more than `job_drain_timeout` later that still waits for the new pool's running job. Each of the three failed compiled before its fix. `test/job_boundaries.rb`, `test/scheduler.rb`, `test/main_roles.rb` and `test/jobs_page.rb` pass unchanged.

### D79. i18n: dictionaries compiled by gen, `t` and `l`, template-path lazy lookup, a locale per thread (2026-11-12, phase 4b)

- Decision, the dictionaries: an application's `config/locales/**/*.yml`, in Rails' shape (`ja: { posts: { index: { title: ... } } }`), are read by `spin run gen` with `Gen::LocaleYaml`, a hand-written reader of the YAML subset of the spec's section 8: a leading `---`, a BOM, CRLF, comments, nested block mappings, plain, single-quoted (`''`) and double-quoted (`\n \t \" \\ \/ \uXXXX`) scalars on one line, `|` and `>` block scalars with `-` / `+` chomping, block sequences of scalars (also at their key's indentation, as rails-i18n writes them), flow `[a, b]` of scalars, and `~`, `null` or nothing as `""`. Every scalar is kept as its text (`3` is `"3"`, `no` is `"no"`, `:year` is `":year"`). Refused, as `path:line: problem`: a tab in indentation, anchors and aliases, tags, a second document, flow mappings, a duplicate key, an unterminated quote, a first key that is not a locale name, a sequence item that is not a scalar, quoted keys, text after a closed quote or flow sequence, a bad `\u` escape, and multi-line plain or quoted scalars ("use | or >"). Files are read sorted by path and a later file's key replaces an earlier one's; a key that is a mapping in one file and a value in another is refused naming both files (`Gen::LocaleScan`). gen writes `gen/locales.rb`: two Hash constants, `Gen::Locales::TEXTS` and `LISTS`, filled at load by `fill_texts_NN` / `fill_lists_NN` methods of at most 50 stores each, `lookup(key)` (a String or nil), `lookup_list(key)` (an `Array<String>`, empty when absent) and the two as lambdas; the file starts with `require "cybertrain/i18n"`, so it loads on its own, and ends with `I18n.install_dictionary(Gen::Locales.resolver, Gen::Locales.list_resolver)`. It is written with empty tables when there is no dictionary, `gen --check` compares it, and the development loop watches `config/locales/**/*.yml`. `cybertrain new` writes `config/locales/en.yml` (Rails' comment block, adapted, and `en: { hello: "Hello world" }`) and commented `i18n_*` lines in `config/app.rb`.
- Decision, the bundled dictionaries: `cybertrain/locales/en.yml` and `ja.yml` hold rails-i18n's keys (commit f5c999b8, MIT, vendored as fetched in `test/fixtures/i18n/rails-i18n/`) cut to what the framework reads (`date`, `time`, `datetime.distance_in_words`, `number`, `errors`, `activerecord.errors.messages`, `helpers.submit`, `support.array`), the framework's own keys under `cybertrain.*` (the jobs page, `paginate`, `cybertrain.helpers.submit.no_model`), and the keys of the screens `cybertrain generate authentication` writes, with `activerecord.attributes.user`. `script/bundle-locales` compiles them with the same reader and emitter into the checked-in `cybertrain/locales/bundled.rb` (`Cybertrain::BundledLocales`), and its `--check` is part of `script/gate`. A key is looked up in the current locale, the application's dictionary first, then in the default locale the same way. The two dictionaries merge as Rails' deep merge of an application file over rails-i18n's does: the application's text replaces whatever the bundled one holds at the key, its plural forms replace a bundled text, and two sets of plural forms merge form by form, so an application that adds only `zero:` keeps the bundled `one:` and `other:`.
- Decision, the API: `I18n` is a top-level module (`cybertrain/i18n.rb`), so application code writes `I18n.t` as in Rails. `I18n.t(key, options)` (also `translate`) takes `default:` (a String, or a Symbol naming another key), `count:` (an Integer, else `ArgumentError`; `zero` for 0 when the key has it, then the locale's category, then `other`), `count_text:` (a String that fills `%{count}` without choosing a form: numericality's limit), `locale:`, and fills every other option into `%{name}` (names are `\w+`; `%%` is a literal `%`; a `%{x}` with no value raises `I18n::MissingInterpolationArgument`). Lists (day and month names) come from `I18n.translate_list`. `I18n.l(time_or_date, format:, locale:)` (also `localize`) reads `time.formats` / `date.formats`, replaces `%a %A %b %B %p %P` with the dictionary's names and hands the rest to `Cybertrain::Strftime.walk`, the per-directive formatter that `time_tag` uses (spikes/NOTES.md rule 96). `I18n.with_locale`, `locale`, `locale=`, `locale_available?`, `default_locale`, `available_locales`, `plural_category`, `human_attribute_name`, `human_model_name`, `error_message` and `full_message` complete it. Its errors (`MissingTranslationData`, `InvalidLocale`, `MissingInterpolationArgument`) subclass `::ArgumentError` directly. In a template, `t` and `l` are helpers: a key starting with `.` is looked up under the rendering template's path, as Rails' virtual path is (`posts/index.html.erb` gives `posts.index`, the partial `posts/_form.html.erb` gives `posts.form`, a component `card_component`, the mail partial `mail/_password_reset.html.erb` `mail.password_reset`), and a key ending in `_html` or `.html` gives HTML with its interpolated values escaped. `Controller#t(".x")` reads `<controller path with . for />.<action>.x`.
- Decision, missing keys: after the default locale, `I18n.t` answers `"translation missing: ja.foo.bar"` outside production (development, test, any other environment) and the humanised last segment (`"Bar"`) in production; the template's `t` answers Rails' `<span class="translation_missing" title="translation missing: ja.foo.bar">Bar</span>` outside production; `config.i18n_raise_on_missing = true` raises `I18n::MissingTranslationData` instead, and a `default:` still answers first. `I18n` cannot read `Config` (it loads on its own under CRuby, as `gen/locales.rb` does), so boot sets `I18n.default_locale`, `available_locales`, `raise_on_missing` and `mark_missing = !production?` in `Runtime.apply`, as it sets `Assets.raise_on_unknown` (D64; `Runtime.reset!` puts them back).
- Decision, the locale: `I18n.locale` is the thread's, `Thread.current[:cybertrain_i18n_locale]` (nil reads as the default locale), cleared at the top of `ContextHandler#call`, `Application#call` and every controller action, so a keep-alive connection's next request, a route miss and an error page start at the default locale; and before every job (`Job::Executor.run_handler`, D68) and scheduled task (`Scheduler.run_task`), whose threads are long-lived, so a job that sets the locale leaves the next one on its thread at the default (Rails' ActiveJob restores the enqueuer's locale around `perform`; here a job that wants one sets it, or uses `I18n.with_locale`). `I18n.locale=` takes a Symbol or a String, compares it with the names of the available locales and never makes a Symbol of request text; any other name raises `I18n::InvalidLocale` (`"\"xx\" is not an available locale"`). `request.preferred_locale(available)` reads `Accept-Language` (the first 4 KB and 32 entries; q-value, then position; a full tag before its language, `ja-JP` matching `:ja`; `q=0`, `*` and junk match nothing) and answers an element of `available`, or `I18n.default_locale`. An application picks the locale in a `before_action` (examples/blog: `?locale=`, then a cookie, then `Accept-Language`). `Config#i18n_default_locale` (`CYBERTRAIN_I18N_DEFAULT_LOCALE`, `:en`), `i18n_available_locales` (`[]`, the default locale alone) and `i18n_raise_on_missing` (false); boot refuses a name that is not a locale name and a default outside a non-empty list.
- Decision, plural rules (CLDR, integer counts, by the locale's language, `pt-BR` by `pt`): en and every unlisted language `one` for 1, else `other`; fr `one` for 0 and 1, `many` for a non-zero multiple of 1,000,000, else `other`; ru `one` (1, 21, 101), `few` (2 to 4, 22 to 24), else `many`; ja and zh `other`.
- Decision, models and views: a validator looks its message up when it runs, along Rails' chain (`activerecord.errors.models.<model>.attributes.<attr>.<type>`, `activerecord.errors.models.<model>.<type>`, `activerecord.errors.messages.<type>`, `errors.attributes.<attr>.<type>`, `errors.messages.<type>`), in the current locale and then the default one; length passes `count:`, which picks rails-i18n's singular ("1 character"), numericality `count_text:`, and every validator the attribute's value as `%{value}` (Rails' interpolation); `message:` still wins. `errors.full_messages` goes through `errors.format` (ja writes no space) with the attribute's human name, and a `:base` message stands alone. gen emits `def self.human_attribute_name(attr, options = {})` per model (`activerecord.attributes.<model>.<attr>`, else the attribute humanised, `author_id` as "Author"); `I18n.human_model_name("admin/post")` reads `activerecord.models.admin/post` and falls back to the last segment humanised, as Rails' `model_name.human` does. Labels read `helpers.label.<object>.<attr>`, then the human attribute name; the submit button `helpers.submit.create` / `update` with the model's human name. The template number helpers read the locale's `number.*` (separators, units, currency), and a setting neither the current nor the default locale holds is en's, as Rails' helpers fall back to their English defaults (so a default locale with no `number.*` keys prints `1,234.5`, not `12345`); a `precision:` given in the call is read as 0.4 read it (its whole part), `time_tag`'s named formats and its default go through `I18n.l` (a String pattern keeps strftime's English names), `time_ago_in_words` and `distance_of_time_in_words` read `datetime.distance_in_words`, and `pluralize` uses the English inflector only when the locale's language is English. `Cybertrain::NumberHelper` called from Ruby keeps its explicit English defaults.
- Reason: Rails' API and rails-i18n's keys, so an application's dictionaries and its `t` calls port as they are; the dictionaries compiled in, so the binary needs no YAML and no files at run time.
- Spinel: no computed `send` or `const_get` (rule 1), so a dictionary is data in a generated Hash, not a YAML file loaded at boot; spike item 6b found a Hash literal of 1,980 pairs taking 12.5 to 15.2 s of cc (rule 101, docs/spinel-feedback.md AI), against 4.7 to 5.4 s for the same Hash filled by 40 methods of 50 stores, which answer in 34 to 52 ns (a `case` built in 4.5 s but took 19 us to miss), hence the fill methods. One return type per name (rules 10 / 34 / 41): Strings from `t` and `lookup`, lists from `translate_list` and `lookup_list`. The dictionary lambdas come from methods and are passed positionally (rules 42 and 82); a stored lambda answering a String or nil, and `with_locale`'s yield into a local with an `ensure`, compile (rule 103). A thread-local Symbol beside the pool's Connection works (rule 102), so the locale needs no `Context` field. Every byte scanner (the YAML reader, the plural rules, `Accept-Language`, the locale-name check) reads through a fresh local (rule 62); messages are built into locals before `raise` (rule 58); the error classes subclass `::ArgumentError` with no same-named class beside them (rule 89).
- Rejected: Psych, even if it compiled (it types scalars, which the dictionaries must keep as text, and `YAML.safe_load` refuses rails-i18n's `:year`); loading YAML at boot (files beside the binary, and a parser in every program); a generated `case` (its arms are tried in order: 19 us for an absent key at 2,000 keys); one Hash literal (rule 101); the spec's controller-and-action lazy lookup (`render :new` from `create` would read `posts.create.*`, where Rails reads `posts.new.*`); a `locale` field on `Context` (the spike found the thread-local works); an `I18n::MISSING` sentinel for absent keys (nil is kept, rule 103); `Post.model_name.human` (`model_name` is already an instance String, rule 34).
- Consequences: English validation messages come from the bundled dictionary, with three changes (spec section 14): a `:base` message is no longer prefixed "Base", an `_id` attribute humanises without "id" ("Article can't be blank"), and a limit of 1 reads "1 character". The ja number formats are rails-i18n's (`"1.23 百万"`, with its space). A missing key in development shows a marked span rather than raising, unless `i18n_raise_on_missing` is on (examples/blog turns it on for its tests). An application that sets `I18n.locale` must list the locale in `i18n_available_locales`. Every bundled lookup in a non-default locale probes the application's dictionary for plural forms, and interpolation always runs: both small, both measured only in the tests.
- Verification: `test/locale_yaml.rb` (the reader and its refusals, rails-i18n's files), `gen_locales.rb` (the merge, the emitter, the standalone load, `--check`, `script/bundle-locales --check`), `i18n.rb` (lookup, interpolation, the five plural rules, missing keys, `with_locale`, two threads, `l`), `i18n_views.rb` (lazy lookup in templates, partials, layouts, components and mails, `_html`, `Controller#t`), `i18n_models.rb` (messages in en and ja, the chain, `:base`, `human_attribute_name`), `helpers.rb` (number helpers, `time_tag`, `pluralize` and distances by locale), `http_request.rb` (`preferred_locale`), `controller.rb` (the reset per action), `config_settings.rb`; spike item 6; examples/blog in English and Japanese.

### D80. Authentication additions: the concern, tokens, password reset, confirmation, lockout, remember me, account pages, admin, upgrade diffs (2026-11-12, phase 4b)

- Decision, the concern: `cybertrain generate authentication` writes `app/controllers/concerns/authentication.rb`, as Rails 8's generator does (`included do before_action :resume_session; before_action :require_authentication; helper_method :authenticated? end`, then private `authenticated?`, `resume_session`, `require_authentication`, `require_admin`, `start_new_session_for(user, remember: false)`, `session_limit`, `terminate_session`, `after_authentication_url` and `mail_link_base`), and inserts `include Authentication` after `ApplicationController`'s class line. `allow_unauthenticated_access(only:, except:)` is a framework class method, `Cybertrain::Controller.allow_unauthenticated_access`, a `skip_before_action(:require_authentication, ...)`: a concern's `class_methods` are defined in `gen/concerns.rb`, which loads after the controllers whose class bodies call it, and its forwarder's `self` would be the concern. A 0.4 application's own `def self.allow_unauthenticated_access` keeps overriding it.
- Decision, tokens: `Cybertrain::Token.issue(purpose, id, expires_in, bound_to, now)` gives `"<id>--<expires>--<64 hex>"`, the hex an HMAC-SHA256 of `"<purpose>:<id>:<expires>:<bound_to>"` under `Crypto.derive_key(secret_key_base, "cybertrain token")`; `Token.id_from(token)` reads the id without verifying (0 when malformed), and `Token.valid?(token, purpose, bound_to, now)` compares the MAC in constant time, then the expiry. The generated `User` looks the record up by `id_from` and checks the token against that record's own binding. A reset token lasts 15 minutes and is bound to `"<password_digest>:<email_address>"`, so a new password or address ends every link sent before; a confirmation token lasts three days (`User::CONFIRMATION_TOKEN_TTL`) and is bound to `"<unconfirmed_email>:<email_address>:<confirmed_at>"`, so it confirms once. A token has no `.` (the router's format split). The pages behind a link answer `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer`, and `ParameterFilter.filter_path` writes a token-shaped path segment as `[FILTERED]` in the log lines written before routing (`Started GET`, `Blocked host`).
- Decision, password reset: `PasswordsController` (`new`, `create`, `edit`, `update`; `resources :passwords, param: :token`), `PasswordsMailer.reset(user, url)` returning a `Cybertrain::MailMessage` sent with `deliver_later`, and its views (the mail's HTML from `mail/_password_reset.html.erb`, its text from the dictionary). `create` answers the same for every address after the same work (a dummy token, the mail rendered for an unsaved `User`), short of queueing it; `update` sets the password, clears a lockout, confirms an account never confirmed (not a new address waiting) and deletes every Session row of the user. Ten requests per client address every three minutes. gen loads `app/mailers/**/*.rb`; `MailMessage#deliver_later` is `Mailer.deliver_later` (the fields are checked at once), `deliver_now` is `Mailer.deliver`.
- Decision, the links in mails: `mail_link_base(mail)` answers the `base_url` setting (`<APP>_BASE_URL`, declared in `config/app.rb` by the generator) when set; else `request.base_url`, but only when `Config#host_check?` (development, or `CYBERTRAIN_HOSTS` set) or in tests; else `""` and `[ERROR] <mail> mail not sent: set <APP>_BASE_URL (or CYBERTRAIN_HOSTS)`, and the page answers as it would have. A forged `Host` never reaches a link.
- Decision, confirmation (`--confirmable`): `users.confirmed_at` and `users.unconfirmed_email`, `ConfirmationsController` (`new` and `create`, the form that mails the link again, and `show`, the link), `ConfirmationsMailer.confirmation_instructions`. A sign-up mails the link and signs nobody in; the right password for an address not confirmed is refused with its own message, the wrong one with the usual message; opening the link confirms and signs nobody in. A new address on the account page waits in `unconfirmed_email` until its own link is opened, and `DELETE /account/pending_email` withdraws it. Users the user command and `/setup` create are confirmed. The concern's `start_new_session_for` also refuses an unconfirmed user (and logs `[WARN] no session started: user <id> has not confirmed its address`), so no controller can sign one in once the concern carries that check: in a new application and in a 0.4 upgrade, where the concern is a new file. When `--confirmable` is added later to an application that already has the concern, the check arrives as the concern's diff, and the output marks that diff `IMPORTANT:` (as it marks the setup controller's, whose first user is otherwise not confirmed). A password reset withdraws a new address waiting (`unconfirmed_email`, and so its link): whoever asked for it may be who the owner is taking the account back from.
- Decision, lockout: `users.failed_attempts` and `users.locked_until`. `User#count_failed_attempt!` counts and locks in one `UPDATE` through `Relation#update_all_sql` (`failed_attempts = CASE WHEN failed_attempts + 1 >= ? THEN 0 ELSE failed_attempts + 1 END, locked_until = CASE ...`), so sign-ins at the same time all count; at `auth_lockout_attempts` (10) the account is locked for `auth_lockout_period` (3600 s). While locked, `User.authenticate_by` verifies against the dummy digest and answers nil, the wrong-password message. A successful sign-in, a reset and the user command clear it; `auth_lockout_attempts = 0` turns lockout off and stops enforcing an existing lock.
- Decision, remember me and the per-session lifetime: `sessions.remember` and `sessions.last_active_at`, and a "Keep me signed in" box (`check_box_tag`, added to the framework). `Session#stamp_lifetime!(seconds, persistent)` writes `"_lifetime"` (seconds) and, for an unremembered sign-in, `"_browser" = "1"`; `SessionStore` reads them in place of `session_absolute_max_age` and `session_max_age`, so a remembered cookie gets the time left as its `Max-Age` (past two weeks) and an unremembered one no `Max-Age` and no `Expires`. `start_new_session_for` calls `restart_lifetime!` (which clears the stamp) and then `stamp_lifetime!(session_limit(remember), remember)`; `session_limit` is `auth_remember_for` (30 days) when remembered, else `session_absolute_max_age`, else `session_max_age`, else two weeks, never 0. `resume_session` ends the Session row past the same limit, or unused for `auth_idle_timeout` (7 days; 0 is off), and touches `last_active_at` at most once a minute.
- Decision, the account page: `AccountsController` (`resource :account, only: [:edit, :update, :destroy]`): a new address or password only with the current password, the account deleted with its sessions in one transaction only with the password. A new password deletes every Session row of the user and signs this browser in again, remembered as it was. Account changes and deletions (so also wrong current passwords) are rate limited per client address and per user (ten every three minutes each), and a wrong current password does not count toward lockout.
- Decision, addresses and sign-up: the generated `User` refuses an address that is not one address, at every save that sets one (sign-up, `/setup`, the account page, the user command, and the address waiting with `--confirmable`): `User.email_address_format?` asks for exactly one `@` with text on both sides and no comma, whitespace, angle bracket or control character, as `Cybertrain::Mailer` splits recipients at commas and one account must be one mailbox, and that `Cybertrain::Mailer.address_problem` finds no problem (plain ASCII, none of `()[];:\"`, the local part at most 64 bytes and the whole at most 254), so no account is saved whose address the mailer would refuse (a Unicode space, a C1 control or `;` would otherwise save, and its mails would fail, only logged). An address saved before the check is not checked again. Some addresses the mail standards allow (a quoted local part with a space, a non-ASCII address) are refused; the method is the application's. Sign-up is rate limited as sign-in is (ten per client address every three minutes): it hashes a password, may mail a link, and its "taken" names registered addresses.
- Decision, admin: `users.admin` (false by default), `User#admin?`, and the concern's `require_admin`, which answers 404 unless the signed-in user is an admin. `./<app> user admin EMAIL` and `user unadmin EMAIL` change it; the user command is written in both modes now. The generator draws the jobs page as `jobs_page "/admin/jobs", before_action: :require_admin, skip_before_action: :require_authentication` (replacing the comment `cybertrain new` writes), so `require_admin` alone answers a visitor there; gen refuses a `skip_before_action:` without a `before_action:` and one naming the `before_action:` itself. When `ApplicationController` defines `require_admin` itself (which then takes the concern's place), the generator warns as it inserts `include Authentication` and draws the jobs page without the skip, so `require_authentication` runs before that method. A `jobs_page` line of the application's own that already skips `require_authentication` counts as drawn; for another, the generator says to add the skip to that line. A failed or discarded mail job's row holds the mail, link included, and the jobs page shows it to an admin: left as is, since the page is for admins, who can act on any account already.
- Decision, text: every string in the generated views, mails and flash messages goes through `t()` (lazy keys in views and actions, full keys in private methods), and the bundled en and ja dictionaries hold them all.
- Decision, re-runs: the edits made at a marker (routes, the layout's nav, `bin/<app>.rb`, `config/app.rb`) are made once: every run looks for them, and a re-run finds them and skips. A file the generator writes whole that exists with other content is left alone and its unified diff printed, headed `manual  <path>: apply this diff by hand, or re-run with --force to overwrite the file`; `patch -p1` applies the whole output. `--force` writes the new file instead. 0.4's block in `ApplicationController` becomes `include Authentication` the same way, `--force` replacing it only when it is exactly as 0.4 wrote it. A `create_*` migration is never rewritten: the columns the existing migrations lack go into one `<v>_add_authentication_columns.rb`, which with `--confirmable` also counts the users already there as confirmed. Until the diffs are applied the application may not build (the new files call what they add), and the output says so.
- Reason: what Rails 8's generator, Devise's recoverable, confirmable, lockable and rememberable, and Mission Control's admin gate give an application, still as code the application owns (D15); the framework adds only what is hard to write (tokens, the per-session lifetime, the atomic update, the masked log).
- Spinel: `rate_limit with:` and `by:` name methods (rule 88: no stored block run through a base class); the concern is expanded by gen (rules 92 and 93), which is why `allow_unauthenticated_access` lives in the framework; one return type per name (rules 10 / 34 / 41) renamed WebSocket's private `Frame#reset` to `clear_message` (it would clash with `PasswordsMailer.reset`), named the mailer method `confirmation_instructions` (`confirm` is `User#confirm`) and gave `MailMessage` the readers `mail_to`, `mail_subject`, `mail_body`, `mail_html`, `mail_from`; token parsing reads through a fresh local (rule 62); `Token` lives beside the template lexer's `Template::Token`, so template code names `Cybertrain::Token`.
- Rejected: a password reset token stored in the table (a signed token bound to the digest needs no column and expires itself); the token in a query parameter rather than Rails' `/passwords/:token/edit` (the path is kept, and the log masks it); the link's base always from the request (a forged `Host` would carry the token to another site); counting lockout in two statements (a timing signal and a race); dummy writes for unknown addresses (more load and code for a signal the per-IP rate limit already bounds); a per-address throttle on mails (per-IP only, as Rails' generator); rewriting a 0.4 application's files (D19: an application's edits are never overwritten without `--force`); a `Current` object (templates read `@current_user`).
- Consequences, accepted and documented in the authentication guide: a wrong password for an existing, unlocked account writes its row once, which an unknown address and a locked account do not (Devise's lockable has the same difference); sign-up, reset and confirmation mails are limited per client address only, so an attacker can mail an address at that rate, and since the sign-in limit (10 every three minutes) equals the lockout threshold, can keep a known address locked from one address; production mails need `<APP>_BASE_URL` (set it with https) or `CYBERTRAIN_HOSTS`; a known address costs the password form one job insert that an unknown one does not (Rails' generator has the same difference); a reset confirms an account never confirmed; the jobs page answers a visitor 404 while an application controller with `before_action :require_admin` sends a visitor to sign in (303), as `require_authentication` runs first there; a `jobs_page` line the application wrote itself is left as it is; the old address is not told of an address change; a pending address never expires (its link does, after three days) and an unconfirmed account is never deleted; with `session_absolute_max_age = 0` an unremembered sign-in still lasts `session_max_age` (or two weeks); `restart_lifetime!` clears a stamp, so it comes first.
- Verification: `test/token.rb`, `mail_message.rb`, `session.rb` (the stamp, the browser cookie, `fixed_now=`), `query_extras.rb` (`update_all_sql`), `middleware.rb` and `host_authorization.rb` (the masked path), `form_tags.rb` (`check_box_tag`), `gen_routes.rb` (`skip_before_action:` and its refusals), `jobs_page.rb`, `controller.rb`, `cli_authentication.rb`, `cli_authentication_upgrade.rb` (against `test/fixtures/auth_04_app.upgrade.diff`); the generated `test/authentication.rb`, `admin.rb`, `remember_me.rb`, `lockout.rb`, `passwords.rb`, `accounts.rb` and `confirmations.rb`; `script/auth-e2e signup invite confirmable upgrade04`, run in CI; examples/blog's tests.

### D81. The clock, durations, time helpers and the configured time zone (2026-11-12, phase 5a)

- Decision, the clock: `Cybertrain::Clock` (cybertrain/clock.rb) is the one seam. `Clock.now` answers a UTC `Time`, real time or the travelled instant; real time is read with `Process.clock_gettime(Process::CLOCK_REALTIME, :microsecond)`, never through `Time.now`, and keeps microseconds; a travel drops the sub-second part (Rails' `with_usec: false`). `Clock.real_now` is real time always (file ages), `Clock.monotonic` the monotonic seconds (waits and durations). The state is two module-level ivars, a flag and Integer microseconds (rules 7, 18, 64). `Time.current` and `Date.current` (rule 87) read it. Every `Time.now` of the framework runtime goes through it (52 sites: timestamps, the cache, rate limits, the session's limits, jobs, tokens, the scheduler, the stream table, the jobs page, `time_ago_in_words`, the mail `Date`, feeds, the health check; the authentication code the generator writes reads `Time.current`); explicit `now` parameters stay. Waits and timeouts (stream hub, WebSocket timers, receivers, the HTTP server's drain, `HttpClient`'s deadlines, the job poll, the supervisor's backoff) and elapsed-time measures use the monotonic clock, so a frozen clock never hangs a wait. The five seams it replaces are removed: `Cache.clock=` / `Cache.system_clock`, `RateLimit.current_time=`, `SessionStore#fixed_now=`, `Application.status_now=` and `Background.supervisor_clock=`. `cybertrain/test/time_now.rb`, which only test programs load (through `cybertrain/test_help`), makes `Time.now` answer `Clock.now`; a production binary keeps the builtin.
- Decision, the test helpers: Rails' `travel_to(time)`, `travel(seconds)`, `freeze_time` and `travel_back`, top-level methods so that both test forms reach them; with a block they forward to `Clock.travel_during`, rule 103's save-yield-ensure in a module function, so nested travels and a raising block restore what they found; without one the travel lasts until `travel_back` or the end of the test (the runner's step 7, D82).
- Decision, durations and calendar methods: `seconds` to `weeks` and the singulars on Integer answer Integer seconds (`5.minutes == 300`; Rails answers a Duration); `ago` / `from_now` answer a Time on the clock. No `month(s)` / `year(s)` on Integer: a month has no length in seconds. On Time: `beginning_of_*` / `end_of_*` (day, week from Monday, month, year), `advance(years:, months:, weeks:, days:, hours:, minutes:, seconds:)` with Integers (the calendar part moves the wall date in the zone with month-end clamping, then hours to seconds add elapsed time; usec kept), `months_ago` / `months_since` / `years_ago` / `years_since`, `yesterday` / `tomorrow`; a skipped wall time moves forward, a repeated one takes the earlier instant (D19's `local_to_utc`). `Cybertrain::Calendar` holds the shared walk (@api private).
- Decision, the zone: `Config#time_zone` (`CYBERTRAIN_TIME_ZONE`, `"UTC"`), an IANA name checked at boot (`time_zone_error`); `Runtime.apply` sets `Clock.zone`, `Runtime.reset!` puts UTC back, `Runtime.describe` ends with `time_zone=`. `Time.zone` answers the `Cybertrain::TimeZone` (its name `zone_name`), `Time.find_zone!(name)` raises `ArgumentError` for an unknown one. Values stay UTC instants: there is no TimeWithZone, `time.hour` is the UTC hour, and the zone decides boundaries, `Date.current` and display. `time.in_time_zone(zone = Time.zone)` answers `Cybertrain::ZonedTime`, a value with the wall fields (`year` ... `wday`, `yday`, `utc_offset`, `zone` the abbreviation, `to_time`, `to_date`) and no arithmetic. `l` and `time_tag` render in the zone (`zone:` for one call): a Time at offset 0, which is every Time the framework hands out, is shown in the zone; a Time carrying another offset of its own keeps it, as Rails renders a plain Time; `zone:` always converts; `time_tag`'s `datetime` carries the zone's offset (`Z` only for UTC itself). `scheduler_tz` follows `time_zone` while unset.
- Reason: Rails' names, so a test that travels and code that reads `Time.current` port as written; one seam instead of five, each with its own spelling and reset; a zone that changes boundaries and display without changing what is stored (D46), which every Time in the framework assumes.
- Spinel: spike item 1 proved that a Ruby-source `def self.now` on `Time` overrides the builtin at every call site, framework files included, so `Time.now` follows a travel in tests; spike item 2 that the Integer and Time methods compile beside every existing namesake (`Cron::Expression#minutes`, `Template::Parser#advance`, ...), two framework-internal methods being renamed (`Net::Timer.format_seconds`, `Stream::Poller.advance_to`), and that `ZonedTime#hour` and its siblings keep Rails' names because the boxed dispatch's default arm calls the builtin Time's method (spikes/NOTES.md rule 106, checked for `hour`, `day`, `wday` and `zone`). `1.month` is refused at build only while no class defines `month`; with `ZonedTime#month` in the program it builds and raises `NoMethodError` when reached (rule 105), so the guide says it fails at run time. No nullable Time ivar (rule 7); the helpers hold no `ensure` themselves (rule 50).
- Rejected: a Ruby-level stub of `Time.now` installed per test (the override is compiled into test programs only; a stub needs run-time redefinition); TimeWithZone (every Time would change type, and with it every column, comparison and `strftime`); `months` / `years` on Integer as 30 / 365 days (silently wrong at month ends); Rails' zone names ("Tokyo"); module functions `Durations.minutes(5)` (the spike's fallback, not needed); keeping the five seams beside the clock.
- Consequences: `1.month.ago` is written `Time.current.months_ago(1)`; `advance` takes Integers only, and in a call that mixes days and hours across an overlap its hours are elapsed time where Rails' TimeWithZone counts wall time; the clock keeps microseconds; an application that sets `time_zone` sees `l`, `time_tag` and its cron tasks move to that zone (UTC before).
- Verification: `test/clock.rb` (the clock, the travel helpers, the scan that no runtime or emitted code reads `Time.now`), `time_travel.rb` (the override with the whole framework), `clock_sites.rb` (the sites follow a travel; waits under `freeze_time`), `durations.rb`, `durations_refused.rb` (`1.month` at run time), `zoned_time.rb`, `helpers.rb` and `i18n.rb` (`l` and `time_tag` in a zone), `scheduler_cron.rb` (`scheduler_tz`'s default), `config_validation.rb`, `runtime.rb`; spike items 1 and 2 (spikes/phase5/).
- Amended 2026-11-13 (after the final review): a registered command (`Main.command`), `./<app> jobs` and `./<app> console` install the configured zone too (`Runtime.apply_zone(config)`, which answers "" or the reason), and refuse an unknown one before the database is opened with boot's line, `error: time_zone "<name>" is not a known time zone`, exit status 1. In a test program `Time.now` answers local time in the process's `TZ`, as the builtin and Rails do, while following a travel (`Clock.now.getlocal`); `Time.current` stays UTC. With `store: :memory`, travelling back before a rate-limit window's start opens a new window. A travel is published to other threads best effort, without a barrier: the scheduler keeps what it remembers across a travel, and a test that travels while live scheduler or worker threads run is not supported. Known limits: `travel_to` takes a Time, not a Date; there is no `Time.zone.local`.

### D82. Test classes, setup and teardown, and database isolation (2026-11-12, phase 5a)

- Decision, the classes: `ActiveSupport::TestCase < Cybertrain::Test::Base`, `ActionDispatch::IntegrationTest < ActiveSupport::TestCase`, `ViewComponent::TestCase < ActiveSupport::TestCase`, in Rails' file layout (`test/models/`, `test/controllers/`, `test/integration/`, ...), loaded by `require "cybertrain/test_help"` from the application's `test/test_helper.rb`. gen rewrites each class-form file (a class inheriting one of them, or an application class that does) into `gen/test/<path>`: `test "name" do` becomes `def test_<name>` (every character outside `[A-Za-z0-9_]` turned into `_`; Rails turns only whitespace into `_`), the `setup do` blocks one `setup` calling `super` first, the `teardown do` blocks one `teardown` running in reverse then `super`, every such method ending in `nil` (one return type, rule 34); line numbers are kept, and the rest of the class body is copied. Whatever the rewrite cannot carry over exactly is refused with `path:line` (since 2026-11-13 by refusing the bundle, not by failing gen, as D83's other refusals) (a non-literal name or one with an escape gen does not read, `\u`, `\x`, `\c`, `\C`, `\M` or octal, a duplicate method name, `setup do` beside `def setup`, `setup { }` / `setup :name`, block parameters, `next` / `break` / `redo`, `return <value>`, a nested or reopened test class, `setup do` in a reopened framework class, a line shaped like a declaration that gen cannot place, mixed indentation); a structural check makes sure every raw line shaped like a declaration is rewritten or refused, and `Test::Base`'s class and instance `test` / `setup` / `teardown` raise if one ever runs unconverted. The top-level form (`test`, top-level `setup` / `teardown` blocks, `Cybertrain::Test.run!`) keeps working, in the bundle and under `spin test`.
- Decision, the order of one test, owned by `cybertrain/test.rb` for both forms (`before_case` / `run_body` / `after_case`, `run_top_level_case`): 1-2 the bundle's clear and fixture load (`before_each_bundled`), 3 the reset hooks (`on_reset`: rate-limit counters, `I18n.locale`, `Mailer.deliveries`, the broadcast floor, the configured zone), 4 setup, 5 the body if nothing before failed, 6 teardown always, 7 `travel_back` and the `after_this_case` callables (receivers' stop, `with_forgery_protection`'s backstop). A failure outside the body makes the case `ERR` with its step named (`in fixtures:`, `in reset:`, `in setup:`); a teardown failure is reported after the body's.
- Decision, isolation: before each test `Test::Database` deletes every row of every table of `db/schema.rb` and of the framework's tables that exist, empties `sqlite_sequence`, and inserts the fixtures, in one transaction with foreign keys off and `PRAGMA foreign_key_check` before the commit. It refuses any database that is not a test database: the environment must be `test` and the connected file `storage/test.sqlite3`, the file `CYBERTRAIN_TEST_DATABASE` names, or a private in-memory one (symlinks resolved, `file:` URIs read by their path, never inside an open transaction).
- Reason: Rails' classes and spelling, so a Rails test file needs few edits; a clear rather than Rails' rollback, because SQLite here has no savepoints (a test's own `transaction` would end a wrapping one), job threads and the stream poller use other connections, and `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` of the framework's tables would roll back with the test.
- Spinel: a block stored on a class and run later through the base class runs the base's stub (rule 88) and cannot see the test's instance variables (rule 2), so gen rewrites blocks into methods and a generated `case` calls them on the concrete class (rule 1); `setup` and `teardown` are ordinary overrides of empty base methods (rule 3); "teardown always runs" is a plain method's `ensure`, never a yielding one's (rules 45, 50, 69); hooks are lambdas made in methods (rule 82); the top-level blocks take no receiver. Spike item 3 compiled the whole shape: an application base class, ivars, a private helper, nested `params:`, a fixture accessor beside a `has_many` of the same name, and the class methods beside the instance ones.
- Rejected: a transaction rolled back per test (above); a copy of a migrated database file per test (a file copy per test, and the pool's connections would need reopening); running class bodies' blocks with `instance_exec` (not compilable); the spike's fallback of the top-level DSL only; silently skipping what the rewrite cannot read.
- Consequences: a later test cannot rely on rows an earlier one made; brace and symbol `setup` forms, `setup do` in a reopened `ActiveSupport::TestCase` and a few other Rails idioms need a small edit; a class-form file runs only through `cybertrain test`; `CYBERTRAIN_TEST_DATABASE` may not name the development or production database (amended 2026-11-13, below).
- Verification: `test/test_lifecycle.rb`, `test_classes.rb` (the order, an inherited base, teardown after a failing setup, assertion and exception), `gen_test_rewrite.rb` (the rewrite and every refusal), `test_database.rb` (the clear, the guard, the failure paths), `receivers.rb` (the automatic stop); the class-form tests of examples/blog and test/fixtures/test_app.
- Amended 2026-11-13 (after the final review): the test method name turns an ASCII character outside `[A-Za-z0-9_]` into `_` and any other character into `_u<lowercase hex code point>_`, closed by `_` (`"ログ"` is `test__u30ed__u30b0_`, `"café x"` is `test_caf_ue9__x`), so names in another script do not collide (a name containing the text `_u3042_` equals the encoded `"あ"`; gen refuses the pair within one class); `-n` still takes the written name. New refusals at gen, each `path:line`: a `def test_x` with parameters, a `def test_x` after `private` or `protected`, a `def setup` / `def teardown` without `super` under an application base class (its `setup do` blocks would not run; refusing is smaller than re-plumbing the chain), and `next` / `break` / `redo` nested in `if`, `unless`, `case` or `begin` of a body. `test"name"` and a tab after `test` are accepted. A `test "x" do` written after `private` is not refused; it becomes a private method and fails when it runs (a known limit). The runner records `NotImplementedError`, other `ScriptError`s and `SystemStackError` from a test, setup, teardown or hook as `ERR <Class>: <message>` and goes on; only `Interrupt`, `SystemExit` and `NoMemoryError` end the run, and under Spinel a real stack overflow still ends the process. The database guard: the bundle marks every database it creates (`PRAGMA application_id` 0x43795472, `Test::Database::TEST_MARKER`) and deletes or empties an existing file only when it carries the mark, is this application's own `storage/test.sqlite3` in any spelling (not through a symlink to another file), or is a zero-byte file with no data in a `-wal` beside it; anything else is refused, exit status 2: `cybertrain test refuses to delete <path>: not a database cybertrain test created; delete it yourself or name another file` (per test: `Test::Database refuses to empty <path>: ...`). A file named `development.sqlite3` or `production.sqlite3` (any letter case), or the same file as `storage/development.sqlite3` / `storage/production.sqlite3`, is refused as `... it is the development database` (or production). The name is a plain path: `:memory:` and `file:` URIs are refused (`cybertrain test refuses CYBERTRAIN_TEST_DATABASE "<value>": name the test database by its file path (not :memory: or a file: URI)`). A custom file created before carries no mark and is refused once; deleting it lets `cybertrain test` create a marked one. A path that becomes a symlink while the database is created is refused, exit 2 (`cybertrain test refuses <path>: it became a symlink while the test database was created (nothing was marked or migrated); remove it and run again`), and the per-test clear names `"main"`, so a TEMP or attached table of the same name is never touched. Known limits: the mark survives a `cp` of a test database; a run killed between create and mark leaves an unmarked file at a named path (refused next time); a directory at `storage/test.sqlite3` raises at delete. No configure block of `config/app.rb` is run for another environment: the scoped re-review found that replaying them ran production-only branches inside the tests, and that a `file:` URI with a query passed the deny list, so a positive mark replaces the list. The bundle deletes the file it tests on, so one wrong export would otherwise destroy production data.

### D83. Fixtures and `cybertrain test`, one program for an application's tests (2026-11-12, phase 5a)

- Decision, fixtures: `test/fixtures/<table>.yml` in Rails' shape, read by gen with D79's YAML subset reader into `gen/test/data.rb`: the table list, one literal `INSERT` per row, and one accessor per file (`articles(:one)` answers `Article.find`, in `ActiveSupport::TestCase` and at the top level; an unknown label raises `Test::FixtureNotFound`). Ids are Rails' `Zlib.crc32(label) % (2**30 - 1)`, computed with a pure-Ruby CRC-32, unless `id:` is given; a `belongs_to` by label sets the foreign key; values are converted by the column's type, datetimes as UTC, and `created_at` / `updated_at` left out are the clock's time at the insert. Refused with `file:line`: ERB, a file that is no table, a key that is neither a column nor a `belongs_to`, a value outside the column's type (an impossible date, an integer past 64 bits, `yes` for a boolean), two labels with one id, a `belongs_to` label that names no row of the parent's file (or a parent row with its own `id:`), a table name that clashes with a harness method or a test helper. `DEFAULTS` is not a row, as in Rails. A fixture gen cannot load does not fail gen (a scaffold's fixture comes before its migration, and `cybertrain db migrate` runs gen first): gen writes a `gen/tests.rb` that only aborts, and `cybertrain test` prints the reason and exits 1.
- Decision, the program: gen writes `gen/tests.rb`, which forces the test environment, requires the framework, `config/app.rb`, gen's outputs, the helper and `test/support/**`, every test file (the class-form copies or the top-level files themselves, each after `Test.begin_file`), then `Gen::Tests`, a generated `case` with one arm per class-form test, boots the application on a fresh database (`Test::Bundle.boot`: the refusal above, forgery protection off and the request log silent unless `config/app.rb` set them, a default mail sender, the database deleted and migrated), and runs. `cybertrain test [FILE|DIR ...] [-n NAME|-n /TEXT/]` checks its arguments, runs `SPIN_DEBUG=1 spin run gen`, compiles `gen/tests.rb` into `build/test/tests` (a release build, `-I gen/test -I test`) only when an input is newer than the binary or the build record beside it (`build/test/tests.inputs`: the command, `spin flags`, the framework's version, spin.toml and spin.lock) differs, and runs it; the arguments filter at run time. The output is a heading per file, a line per test with the test's own line on a failure, then the totals; the exit status is 1 on a failure, 2 on bad arguments or a refused database. A bundle that cannot be built (a top-level `def` or constant in two files, a top-level `def render_inline`, the nested block forms, a top-level declaration in a support file) is refused the same way as a broken fixture (pre-flight ruling B2), so `spin run gen` and `spin test` of existing applications keep working through these bundle-level refusals. Since the final review (2026-11-13) a refusal of the class-form rewrite (D82) is one of them too: gen exits 0 and writes the refused program, and `cybertrain test` prints `<path>:<line>: <reason>` and exits 1.
- Reason: one compile for every test instead of one per file, which an application with fifty files could not afford; fixtures as in Rails, compiled in, since there is no `eval` and no YAML at run time.
- Spinel: no computed `send` (rule 1), hence the generated `case` and the generated accessors; top-level methods and constants of several files merge in one program (rules 10, 41), hence the refusal; spike item 3 gave the compile command; spike item 6 measured the framework's own suite as one bundle: 196 programs passed the front end in about 6.5 min at about 1.65 GB and wrote 87.6 MB of C, whose compile was killed by the OOM killer at 9.7 GB after about 21 min on a 16 GB shared machine (2026-11-12; docs/spinel-feedback.md AK), which is why the framework's suite stays one program per file (D34).
- Measured (not budgets; a shared 4-vCPU cloud machine, figures depend on the machine): the blog's application with three bundled test files of eight tests compiled in 162 to 233 s at a peak of 2.2 GB into an 8.1 MB binary, and the bundle of `script/test-command-e2e`'s test_app in about 2 minutes (2026-11-12); examples/blog's 149 tests and 1489 assertions in 21 files compiled in about 527 s the first time, the whole first `cybertrain test` took 656 s, and the binary is 10.2 MB; a later `cybertrain test test/controllers` with nothing changed reused the binary and started in about 1 s (2026-11-13, the blog task's own run). Editing any test compiles the whole program again, and spin rebuilds `bin/gen` first (about 55 s) because a test file is one of its inputs (spikes/NOTES.md rule 108).
- Rejected: one program per test file (the compile cost above); loading YAML at run time; ERB in fixtures (no `eval`); Rails' `fixtures :articles` subsets (every file is loaded for every test); a fixture problem failing gen (it would break `cybertrain db migrate` after a scaffold); bundling the framework's suite (the C compile's memory).
- Consequences: an edit to one test costs a full compile; a file argument and `-n` narrow the run, not the build; a parent row referenced by a child fixture cannot be destroyed (as in Rails); `gen/tests.rb` and `gen/test/` are committed and checked by `gen --check`.
- Verification: `test/gen_fixtures.rb` (ids, labels, every column type, the refusals), `test_bundle_fixtures.rb`, `gen_tests_bundle.rb` (the program, the refusals), `test_bundle.rb`, `cli_test_command.rb` (arguments, staleness, the build record, symlinked roots); `script/test-command-e2e` (ten variants over test/fixtures/test_app); examples/blog's `cybertrain test`.
- Amended 2026-11-13 (after the final review): `cybertrain test` deletes only a database it marked (D82's guard; this reverses the earlier note that `CYBERTRAIN_TEST_DATABASE` could name any file). `-n /TEXT/` is a plain substring (`.`, `?` and `()` literal); TEXT with `^ $ \ * + [ ] { } |` is refused with exit status 2; `FILE:LINE` is refused (`line filters are not supported; give the file, and -n for the name`); `--` ends the options; a filter that keeps no test prints `no test matched the filter` and exits 0. The build record lists the files under `test/`, so adding or deleting one compiles again. The test environment is set unless `config/app.rb` sets it: mail `:test` and `force_ssl` off (their variables are not read, even when invalid), no host check from an exported `CYBERTRAIN_HOSTS`, `www.example.com` always an allowed host, uploads under `tmp/test_uploads/`, emptied at every run. A class-form rewrite refusal now refuses the bundle, as above. Known limits: a `def setup` in a module is not checked for `super`; a class two levels under an application base whose base defines no `setup` is still asked for `super`; while a test tree is refused, `gen --check` does not compare the stale copies in `gen/test/` (CI fails at `cybertrain test`). Measured 2026-11-13: examples/blog 149 tests, 1507 assertions; a scratch application with `generate authentication` and `scaffold post user:references`, 66 tests, 822 assertions.

### D84. Component, stream, mail and job test helpers, the new assertions and the receivers' automatic stop (2026-11-12, phase 5a)

- Decision, requests: `ActionDispatch::IntegrationTest` has `get`, `post`, `patch`, `put`, `delete`, `head` (path, `params:`, `headers:`), each answering the `Cybertrain::Response` (Rails answers the status), `response`, `follow_redirect!`, `cookies`, `assert_response`, `assert_redirected_to`, `fixture_file_upload` (multipart), the application's path helpers, and a new `Test::Client` per test. `params:` takes nested Hashes and typed values sent as a form would (a Date, a Time, true / false, numbers, nil). A URL on the application's host is reduced to its path, in requests and on both sides of `assert_redirected_to`; another host is refused. Forgery protection is off unless the application set `csrf`; `with_forgery_protection { }` turns it on for a block, put back in `ensure` and again after the test. `assert_response :error` is exactly 500 (an earlier decision; Rails takes any 5xx). Not offered: `session`, `flash`, `assigns`, `request`, `host!`, `as:`, a client address; a second browser is the generated `SessionTestHelper#open_session`.
- Decision, components and HTML: `render_inline(component, content: "")` and `render_inline(partial:, locals:)` answer the HTML String and keep it; `assert_select(selector, equality = nil, count:, minimum:, maximum:, text:)` over the last render or response of this test (none fails: "No response yet"), with Rails' selector subset (tags, `#id`, `.class`, `[attr]`, `[attr="v"]`, compounds, descendant and child combinators, lists), every other form failing by name, Rails' positional second argument (no Range), and `text:` compared after collapsing whitespace and stripping (Rails strips only). The reader, `Test::HtmlReader`, reads well-formed HTML as written: no `<tbody>` inserted, `<p><div>` nested, an omitted end tag and `<div/>` refused with the byte offset. Every framework template parses.
- Decision, streams: `assert_broadcasts(stream, count) { }` and `assert_no_broadcasts` count `cybertrain_messages` rows above a floor read before the block or when the test began (nothing recorded, no production code touched); `assert_turbo_stream(action:, target:, count: 1)` reads the last response, which must be `text/vnd.turbo-stream.html` (turbo-rails' check), or render, with `<template>` content blanked.
- Decision, mail and jobs: `Config#mail_delivery_method` (`CYBERTRAIN_MAIL_DELIVERY_METHOD`), `:test` in the test environment and `:smtp` elsewhere; `:test` checks each message and appends it to `Mailer.deliveries`, emptied before every test; outside the test environment it boots with a warning (Rails allows it). The bundle gives mail the sender `from@example.com` unless the application set one. `assert_emails(n) { }` / `assert_no_emails` deliver the mail queued in the block first and fail with a failed mail job's error. `perform_enqueued_jobs { }` performs every job queued in the block and the jobs those queue, whatever their `run_at`, each once; a job that raises fails the test with its class and error, and a `discard_on` discard counts as run.
- Decision, assertions: `assert_in_delta`, `assert_match` / `assert_no_match`, `assert_not_equal`, `assert_not_nil`, `assert_not_includes`, `assert_empty` / `assert_not_empty`, `assert_not`, `assert_nothing_raised`, lambda-only `assert_difference` / `assert_no_difference` / `assert_changes` (Rails' check order, `from:` / `to:` defaulting to a sentinel), Minitest's failure texts, and `assert_raises(Class)` beside the String form, matched along the superclass chain, with `rescue Exception` and non-StandardErrors raised on unless expected, answering the exception's message String (Rails answers the exception). Each assertion is one `Test.check_*` shared by its forms.
